B) Source Generators run at compile time to generate extra code based on syntax analysis (e.g. System.Text.Json, Regex
Generator); Interceptors (C# 12, experimental in .NET 8) allow replacing method calls with generated code (used for minimal
API AOT); Inline arrays [InlineArray(10)] struct Buffer { int _element; } provide fixed-size struct arrays without heap
C) Source generators run at runtime
D) Interceptors are stable public API in .NET 9 for production
Q30. How do you implement zero-allocation string building and UTF8
string literals in .NET 8/9?
A) Use + concatenation always
B) Use DefaultInterpolatedStringHandler via interpolated string handler pattern, StringBuilder with Span<char> pooling,
stackalloc char[256], and new C# 11+ UTF8 literals producing ReadOnlySpan<byte> via u8 suffix; .NET 8+ has

--- PAGE 94 ---
SearchValues<T> and CompositeFormat for optimized formatting
C) UTF8 literals not exist
D) stackalloc always heap allocates
Answer Keys
| Question | Correct Answer | Brief Reasoning |
|----------|----------------|-----------------|
| Q1 | B | Struct value heap distinction correct |
| Q2 | B | Nullable reference semantics |
| Q3 | C | var int inference |
| Q4 | B | sealed prevents inheritance |
| Q5 | B | String immutability |
| Q6 | C | do-while at least once |
| Q7 | B | Using declaration disposal |
| Q8 | A | try-catch-finally mechanics |
| Q9 | B | Collection expressions C#12 |
| Q10 | B | == overloaded for string |
| Q11 | B | Task vs ValueTask allocation |
| Q12 | B | IEnumerable vs IQueryable SQL translation |
| Q13 | B | record vs record struct equality |
| Q14 | B | Primary constructors C#12 |
| Q15 | B | Span zero-allocation view |
| Q16 | B | async void fire-and-forget danger |
| Q17 | B | Property pattern matching |
| Q18 | B | DI lifetimes scoped vs singleton |
| Q19 | B | required keyword enforcement |
| Q20 | B | Native AOT limitations |
| Q21 | B | ConfigureAwait context capture |
| Q22 | B | IAsyncEnumerable streaming + cancellation |
| Q23 | B | FrozenDictionary read-optimized |
| Q24 | B | Channel async producer-consumer |
| Q25 | B | ref/in/ref field C#12 |
| Q26 | B | lock vs Semaphore vs Mutex |
| Q27 | B | params Span + field keyword C#13 |
| Q28 | B | Server vs Workstation + DATAS |
| Q29 | B | Generators vs Interceptors vs InlineArray |
| Q30 | B | Zero-alloc string building + u8 literals |
Detailed Explanations
Q1. Explanation
Option B is correct because struct is a value type with copy-by-value semantics and cannot inherit, while class is reference
type on heap. This distinction impacts performance, equality, and memory allocation as structs can live inline or on stack.
Q2. Explanation

--- PAGE 95 ---
A string? explicitly opts-in to nullable annotation context, enabling compiler flow analysis to warn on possible null dereference.
With NRT enabled in .NET 6+ projects by default, this prevents NullReferenceException by static analysis.
Q3. Explanation
C# var uses target type inference from RHS at compile time, so 5 is int literal, thus x is int. It is statically typed, not dynamic,
unlike JavaScript var.
Q4. Explanation
sealed prevents further inheritance and enables devirtualization optimizations by JIT. Marking library types sealed also
improves Native AOT and trim analysis in .NET 8+.
Q5. Explanation
System.String is immutable for security and interning reasons. Methods like ToUpper() allocate new string, leaving original
reference unchanged, crucial for thread safety.
Q6. Explanation
do { } while(condition); checks condition after body, guaranteeing one execution. Other loops evaluate condition first and may
skip entirely.
Q7. Explanation
Using declarations were introduced in C# 8 to remove nesting: compiler inserts Dispose() call at end of enclosing block scope
via try-finally. For async disposables, use await using.
Q8. Explanation
Correct exception handling uses try-catch for specific types, avoid catching System.Exception blindly, and finally for cleanup.
Finally is not called only on fail-fast or corrupted process.
Q9. Explanation
C# 12 collection expressions [1,2,3] can target array, List, Span, etc., inferred by target type, with spread operator ..other. It
unifies collection initialization syntax introduced in .NET 8 SDK.
Q10. Explanation
string overloads == to call Equals ordinally, unlike default reference equality for other classes. Calling s1.Equals(s2) when s1
is null throws, while s1 == s2 safely handles nulls and calls overloaded operator.
Q11. Explanation
ValueTask<T> avoids Task allocation when result is immediately available, common for cache hits and parsing, used heavily
in System.IO.Pipelines. However awaiting same ValueTask twice or mixing sync access is illegal due to potential pool reuse.
Q12. Explanation
EF Core IQueryable provider translates expression tree to SQL via visitors; adding .Where() before ToList keeps filter
server-side. Calling .AsEnumerable() prematurely switches to client-side LINQ and can cause N+1 or full table loads.
Q13. Explanation
Records provide synthesized Deconstruct, ToString, equality by EqualityContract, and non-destructive mutation via with.
Record struct defaults to mutable fields but equality remains value-based, and readonly record struct gives immutability
similar to readonly struct.

--- PAGE 96 ---
Q14. Explanation
Primary constructors simplify dependency injection and DTOs: parameters are in scope for field initializers and entire class
body, with mutable capture. Compiler generates backing fields for captured parameters used outside constructor, reducing
boilerplate vs C# 11 record primary ctor.
Q15. Explanation
Span<T> is ref struct guaranteeing no heap promotion, enabling safe pointer-like slicing over stackalloc, arrays, or
unmanaged memory. Introduced in .NET Core 2.1, it underpins zero-allocation libraries like System.Text.Json and Kestrel.
Q16. Explanation
Async void is needed for ICommand and event handlers where signature forces void, but awaiters cannot observe completion
so exceptions go to SynchronizationContext.Unobserved and terminate. Prefer Task return and use async Task void only at
UI boundaries.
Q17. Explanation
Recursive pattern matching allows combining type, property, and relational patterns concisely, reducing nested ifs. The {
Count: >0 } is a property pattern with relational pattern >0, added in C# 9/10 to improve guard clauses.
Q18. Explanation
The container tracks lifetimes; injecting Scoped service into Singleton causes captive dependency - Scoped instance lives as
long as Singleton, leaking data across requests. Use IServiceScopeFactory to create scope manually from singleton.
Q19. Explanation
required enforces definite assignment at object creation point, checked by compiler via dataflow; RequiredMember and
SetsRequiredMembers allow constructors to satisfy members. Useful for non-nullable properties without forced
parameterized ctor in EF and serializers.
Q20. Explanation
Native AOT requires static analyzability; reflection like Type.GetType(string) or Assembly.Load may fail unless annotated with
DynamicallyAccessedMembers. Libraries must be trimmable-safe and AOT-compatible, enforced via IsAotCompatible
property and warnings IL2026, IL3050.
Q21. Explanation
ASP.NET Core removed SynchronizationContext to avoid deadlocks present in legacy ASP.NET where blocking on async
.Result caused context capture deadlock. Libraries targeting multi-platform still use ConfigureAwait(false) to avoid resume on
UI thread for performance.
Q22. Explanation
IAsyncEnumerable enables push-pull async streaming ideal for database cursors and gRPC streams; cancellation token
propagation requires [EnumeratorCancellation] on token parameter to flow to GetAsyncEnumerator. Without
WithCancellation, enumeration cannot be cancelled gracefully mid-stream.
Q23. Explanation
FrozenDictionary builds perfect hashing or ordinal ignore-case optimized trie at creation cost to make reads ~20-40% faster
for large dictionaries. Used in .NET 8 for ObjectPool, regex, and minimal API route tables where dictionary is static after
startup.

--- PAGE 97 ---
Q24. Explanation
Channel offers two sides ChannelReader/ChannelWriter, supporting async waits via Task and completion propagation;
BoundedChannelOptions handles backpressure via Wait, DropNewest, etc. This matches Go channels and is base for
System.Threading.Channels in ASP.NET Core response pipelines.
Q25. Explanation
Ref fields enable ref struct to store by-ref safely only within ref struct, enforcing scoped ref rules (scoped ref) to prevent
escaping to heap. This enabled Span<T>-like internal implementation and allows custom ref-safe slices without unsafe, new
in C# 12.
Q26. Explanation
lock in .NET 9 was upgraded to use System.Threading.Lock type for better diagnostics; it cannot be held across await. Async
coordination should use SemaphoreSlim.WaitAsync or AsyncLock patterns; Mutex incurs kernel transition ~1000x slower
than Monitor.
Q27. Explanation
C# 13 params generalization removes array allocation for params Span<int> allowing Print(1,2,3) to pass via InlineArray
stack allocation. Field keyword solves auto-prop setter logic without explicitly declaring backing field, completing semi-auto
props introduced in preview .NET 9.
Q28. Explanation
Server GC default in ASP.NET Core uses many heaps for throughput but high memory; DATAS (enabled via
DOTNET_GCName or config) adjusts heap hard limits based on actual usage, reducing memory in Kubernetes with memory
limits. dotnet-counters can monitor GC generations and pause time.
Q29. Explanation
Generators are ISourceGenerator/IncrementalGenerator run by compiler, no runtime overhead, used for interop marshalling.
Interceptors were promoted as [InterceptsLocation] to reroute calls, critical for minimal APIs to generate static route handlers
without reflection under AOT. InlineArray gives C-style fixed buffer with safe Span access.
Q30. Explanation
Modern .NET avoids allocations via string.Create with SpanAction, InterpolatedStringHandler which writes directly to buffer
from format provider, and u8 suffix for compile-time UTF-8 bytes. SearchValues.Create uses vectorized SIMD (AVX2/Arm
Neon) for fast char searching vs manual loops.

--- PAGE 98 ---
Course File: 15_Golang.md
# Go (Golang) - Technical Proficiency Exam
**Total Questions: 30 | Duration: 90 Minutes | Passing Score: 70%**
**Exam Covers: Go 1.21 - Go 1.23 (2024-2026 standards)**
Part 1: Beginner Level (Questions 1-10)
Q1. What is the zero value of a string in Go?
A) nil
B) "" (empty string)
C) "zero"
D) Compiler error
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Every type in Go has a zero value. For strings, the zero value is an empty string "", not nil. Strings are value
types, not reference types.
Q2. Which keyword is used to declare a constant in Go?
A) const
B) constant
C) let
D) final
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** The const keyword declares immutable values at compile time. Constants can be typed or untyped and must
be determinable at compile time.
Q3. How do you declare a variable with short declaration syntax inside a
function?
A) var x = 10
B) x := 10
C) x = 10
D) let x = 10
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The := operator is the short variable declaration that infers type and declares. It can only be used inside
functions, not at package level.
Q4. What does go mod init do?
A) Installs all dependencies
B) Initializes a new Go module and creates a go.mod file
C) Compiles the Go program
D) Formats Go code
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** go mod init <module-path> initializes a module which is the unit for dependency management. It creates a
go.mod file that tracks the module path and its dependencies.

--- PAGE 99 ---
Q5. Which built-in function returns the length of a slice, map, or string?
A) size()
B) length()
C) len()
D) count()
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** len() is a built-in function that returns length for strings, slices, arrays, maps, and channels. For channels it
returns number of elements queued.
Q6. What is the correct way to define a function in Go?
A) function foo() {}
B) def foo():
C) func foo() {}
D) fn foo() {}
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Go uses the func keyword to declare functions. Functions are first-class citizens and can be defined at
package level or as methods with receivers.
Q7. Arrays and slices in Go - which statement is true?
A) Arrays and slices are identical
B) Array size is part of its type, slice is a dynamic view over an array
C) Slices have fixed size, arrays are dynamic
D) You cannot append to a slice
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In Go, [3]int and [4]int are different types because size is part of array type. A slice is a descriptor containing
pointer, length, and capacity, providing a dynamic view.
Q8. What will make([]int, 3, 5) create?
A) Slice with length 3 and capacity 5
B) Slice with length 5 and capacity 3
C) Array with 3 elements
D) Compiler error - invalid arguments
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** make for slices takes type, length, and optional capacity. Here it creates a slice with 3 zero-valued elements
but underlying array capacity of 5, allowing efficient appends.
Q9. How do you handle errors idiomatically in Go?
A) Using try-catch blocks
B) Returning error as last return value and checking if err != nil
C) Using exceptions
D) Ignoring errors
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go does not have exceptions; errors are values. The idiomatic pattern is to return error as the last value and
explicitly check it, promoting visible error handling.

--- PAGE 100 ---
Q10. What does defer do?
A) Executes a function immediately
B) Schedules a function call to run after the surrounding function returns
C) Deletes a variable
D) Pauses execution
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** defer pushes a call onto a LIFO stack executed when the enclosing function returns. It is commonly used for
resource cleanup like closing files or unlocking mutexes.
Part 2: Intermediate Level (Questions 11-20)
Q11. What is the difference between a buffered and unbuffered channel?
A) No difference
B) Unbuffered channel requires both sender and receiver ready simultaneously; buffered allows sends up to capacity without
blocking
C) Buffered channels are faster always
D) Unbuffered channels can only send integers
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Unbuffered channels (capacity 0) provide synchronous communication with rendezvous semantics. Buffered
channels decouple sender/receiver up to capacity, blocking only when full or empty.
Q12. Consider this code:
type S struct { m sync.Mutex }
func (s S) Lock() { s.m.Lock() }
What is wrong?
A) Nothing wrong
B) Value receiver copies the Mutex, so locking has no effect on original
C) sync.Mutex cannot be embedded
D) Method must be private
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** sync.Mutex must not be copied after first use – vet warns about this. Using a value receiver copies the whole
struct including mutex, so unlocked original leads to data races.
Q13. What does the context package provide, introduced to prevent
goroutine leaks?
A) Only cancellation signal
B) Cancellation, timeout/deadline, and request-scoped values propagation across API boundaries and goroutines
C) Database context
D) Only logging
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** context.Context is standard for carrying deadlines, cancellation signals, and request-scoped values.
Functions should accept Context as first argument and respect ctx.Done() to avoid leaked goroutines.
Q14. In Go 1.21+, which loop variable behavior changed with loopvar
experiment now default?

--- PAGE 101 ---
A) Loop variables are now per-iteration, fixing closure capture bug in goroutines
B) Loop variables are now global
C) No change
D) Loops are now parallel
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Before Go 1.22, loop variables were reused per loop, causing classic bug where goroutines captured same
variable. Since Go 1.22 (previewed in 1.21 with LOOPVAR), each iteration creates new variable, making go func(){ use(v) }()
safe.
Q15. What is interface nil vs typed nil pitfall?
func returnsNil() error {
var p *MyError = nil
return p
What does caller see?
A) Caller sees nil interface
B) Caller sees non-nil interface containing typed nil pointer, so err != nil is true
C) Compile error
D) Panic
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** An interface value is nil only when both type and value are nil. Returning a typed nil pointer as error creates
non-nil interface with dynamic type *MyError, causing nil-check to pass unexpectedly.
Q16. How do generics (since Go 1.18) declare a constrained type
parameter?
A) func Foo[T any](x T)
B) func Foo<T>(x T)
C) generic Foo[T]
D) func Foo[T comparable](x T) is only valid syntax
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Go generics use [T constraint] syntax. any (alias for interface{}) allows any type, comparable allows ==
comparable types. Option A shows correct generic syntax, D is also valid but A is the canonical example.
Q17. What is sync.WaitGroup used for?
A) To wait for a collection of goroutines to finish
B) To lock memory
C) To wait for HTTP response
D) To garbage collect
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** WaitGroup is a counter: Add() increments, Done() decrements, Wait() blocks until counter zero. It is the
standard way to wait for multiple goroutines, unlike manual channel signaling.
Q18. slices package added in Go 1.21 - what does slices.Clone and
slices.Delete do?
A) Both are unsafe operations
B) Clone makes a shallow copy of slice; Delete removes elements at range [i,j) without leaking if handled

--- PAGE 102 ---
C) Delete always causes memory leak
D) Clone is deep copy for structs
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go 1.21 introduced slices and maps standard packages. slices.Clone returns a copy of slice elements
(shallow). slices.Delete removes range and zeros tail to avoid retaining references, preventing memory leaks.
Q19. What is the correct way to avoid data race when multiple goroutines
write to a map?
A) Maps are thread-safe by default
B) Use sync.Mutex or sync.RWMutex to guard map, or use sync.Map for specific use cases
C) Use atomic package on map
D) Just use buffered channel as map
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go maps are not safe for concurrent write or concurrent read+write; runtime will panic. You must synchronize
with mutex/RWMutex, or use sync.Map which is optimized for mostly-read, disjoint keys, or cache.
Q20. Explain io.Reader and io.Writer interfaces.
type Reader interface { Read(p []byte) (n int, err error) }
type Writer interface { Write(p []byte) (n int, err error) }
Why are they powerful?
A) They are just for files
B) They enable composition - any type implementing them works with all stdlib tools (http, compress, crypto, json)
C) They are deprecated since Go 1.20
D) Only os.File implements them
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** These tiny interfaces demonstrate Go's composition philosophy. Because they are small, many types
implement them, and functions like io.Copy, json.NewDecoder(r), or gzip.NewReader(r) work with any source.
Part 3: Advanced Level (Questions 21-30)
Q21. Go scheduler GMP model (Go 1.19-1.23): What happens when a
goroutine does blocking syscall?
A) Entire OS thread (M) blocks and scheduler cannot reuse it
B) G is detached, M blocks, P migrates to another M or new M created to continue running other Gs - sysmon handles
preemption
C) Program crashes
D) Garbage collector stops
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In GMP model, P (processor) is detached from M doing blocking syscall. Scheduler assigns P to another idle
M or creates new M. Since Go 1.14+ async preemption via signals prevents unbounded blocking from hogging P.
Q22. Consider escape analysis:
func foo() *int { x := 42; return &x }
Why does this work in Go but would be stack-use-after-return bug in C?
A) It doesn't work, it panics

--- PAGE 103 ---
B) Compiler's escape analysis moves x to heap because its address escapes function scope
C) Go copies stack to heap on return
D) Integers are always on heap
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go compiler performs escape analysis at compile time. If address of local variable escapes (returned, sent to
channel, stored globally), compiler allocates it on heap with GC management. Run go build -gcflags=-m to see escapes.
Q23. What is the behavior of select with nil channels in Go 1.21+?
A) Panics
B) Case with nil channel blocks forever and is never selected, useful for disabling cases dynamically
C) Selects immediately
D) Compile error
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Sending or receiving on nil channel blocks forever. In select, a nil channel case is ignored. Idiom: set channel
variable to nil to disable that case without complicated if logic, e.g., if done { ch = nil }.
Q24. Go memory model and atomics: Why was atomic.Pointer[T]
introduced in Go 1.19 and when to use sync/atomic vs mutex?
A) Mutex always better
B) atomic.Pointer[T] provides type-safe atomic pointer operations; use atomics for single-word counters/flags where
contention low and overhead matters, mutex for critical sections with multiple invariants
C) Atomics are deprecated
D) No difference
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Before 1.19, atomic.Value and unsafe.Pointer tricks were needed for pointer CAS. atomic.Pointer[T] is
generic type-safe. Atomics avoid scheduler involvement and are cheaper for single variable, but don't compose for
multi-variable consistency.
Q25. Explain clear builtin added in Go 1.21 and its relation to maps/slices:
A) Only clears variables to zero value
B) clear(m) deletes all entries from map; clear(s) zeros all elements of slice but keeps len/cap; helps clear references for GC
C) Freezes the map
D) Same as make
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go 1.21 added clear as builtin mirroring slices/maps package. For maps it deletes all keys (map becomes
empty but non-nil). For slices it sets each element to zero value, which is critical to drop pointers and allow GC.
Q26. What is the difference between context.WithValue misuse vs proper
use? Which causes memory leak in 2024+?
A) No difference
B) Storing large structs or using string keys without custom type leaks; proper use is small request-scoped values with
custom unexported key type, never for optional params
C) WithValue always leaks
D) Keys must be integers
**Answer: B**

--- PAGE 104 ---
**Explanation:** Contexts form a chain holding parent references until cancelled. Putting large blobs in context keeps them
alive for entire request lifetime. Use unexported custom type as key to avoid collisions, and only for cross-cutting data like
requestID, not function args.
Q27. Go 1.20+ introduced arena experiment and improved GC. How does
Pacer and GOGC work since Go 1.19?
A) GOGC deprecated
B) GOGC is percentage of heap growth causing next GC; pacer in 1.19+ aims for less CPU spikes, GOMEMLIMIT limits total
memory; arena would provide manual region-based allocation
C) GC runs every second
D) No pacer
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** GC pacer redesigned in Go 1.19 to minimize assist time and pacing errors. GOGC=100 means heap can
grow 100% before GC. Since Go 1.19, GOMEMLIMIT caps application memory, triggering GC more aggressively to stay
within limit.
Q28. Subtle for range over integer added in Go 1.22 - and performance of
defer in loop?
A) for i := range 10 not valid
B) Go 1.22 allows for i := range n iterating 0 to n-1; defer inside loop stacks and executes only at function exit, causing
resource buildup - should wrap in func or use Go 1.21+ explicit cleanup pattern
C) Defer in loop executes each iteration
D) Range over int panics
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Go 1.22 added integer range: for i := range 10 equals 0..9, and for range 10 repeats 10 times. Defer inside
loops is classic perf bug: defers don't run per iteration, they accumulate and run at return, holding resources longer than
intended.
Q29. How does runtime.SetFinalizer interact with finalizers, and what is the
pattern for io.Closer wrapper since Go 1.21 log/slog and
context.AfterFunc?
A) SetFinalizer guarantees immediate cleanup
B) Finalizers are non-deterministic, may not run, cause heap retention and GC overhead; avoid - use runtime.AddCleanup
(Go 1.24 proposal, available as context.AfterFunc / Cleanuper pattern) or explicit Close; AfterFunc registers function when
context done
C) Finalizer replaces defer
D) Finalizer is sync
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Finalizers are best-effort, run in separate goroutine after object unreachable, may resurrect object, delaying
collection. They cause latency. Go discourages them. Go 1.21 added context.AfterFunc and Go 1.23 improved slog resource
cleanup; newer AddCleanup proposal formalizes explicit cleanup attachment.
Q30. Design a rate limiter and singleflight in Go for 10k RPS service -
which primitives to use?
// Need: limit 10k req/s globally, coalesce duplicate in-flight requests
A) Use time.Sleep only

--- PAGE 105 ---
B) Use golang.org/x/time/rate token bucket for rate limiting + golang.org/x/sync/singleflight to deduplicate concurrent identical
calls, combined with context cancellation
C) Mutex only
D) Channel only
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** rate.Limiter implements token bucket with burst and sustained RPS, supports context-aware Wait/Reserve.
singleflight.Group suppresses duplicate calls where many goroutines request same keyII - only one execution runs, others
share result, critical for cache stampede prevention at high RPS.

# Exam 16: Java - Core to Modern Java (Java 17-24)
This exam covers Java fundamentals, OOP, collections, modern features from Java 17-24 (records, sealed classes, pattern
matching, virtual threads), and concurrency.
Beginner Level - 10 Questions
**1. What is the difference between JDK, JRE, and JVM?**
A) JDK is for running apps, JRE for developing, JVM is a database
B) JVM executes bytecode, JRE = JVM + libraries to run apps, JDK = JRE + development tools (compiler, debugger)
C) They are all the same
D) JDK is old name for JRE
**2. Which of the following is the correct way to declare the main method in Java?**
A) public void main(String[] args)
B) public static void main(String[] args)
C) static public void Main(String args[])
D) public static void main()
**3. What will happen with this code? String s1 = "java"; String s2 = new String("java"); System.out.println(s1 == s2);**
A) true
B) false
C) Compilation error
D) NullPointerException
**4. Which primitive types in Java is correct about memory size?**
A) int is 8 bytes, long is 4 bytes
B) int is 32-bit (4 bytes), long is 64-bit (8 bytes), boolean size is JVM dependent
C) All primitives are 4 bytes
D) char is 1 byte in Java
**5. What does final mean when applied to a variable, method, and class respectively?**
A) Variable cannot be reassigned, method cannot be overridden, class cannot be subclassed
B) Variable is constant across all instances, method is abstract, class is immutable
C) Same meaning for all: cannot be changed
D) Variable is thread-safe, method is static, class is finalizable
**6. What is the default value of an instance field of type int and Integer if not initialized?**
A) Both are 0
B) int is 0, Integer is null
C) Both are null
D) Compilation error if not initialized
**7. Which statement about == vs .equals() is true?**
A) Both compare content
B) == compares reference identity for objects, .equals() compares logical equality (if overridden)
C) .equals() always compares references
D) You should always use == for Strings
**8. Which access modifiers make a member visible only within its own package and subclasses in other packages?**
A) private
B) default (package-private)

--- PAGE 108 ---
C) protected
D) public
**9. What happens when you attempt to modify a List created by List.of(1,2,3)?**
A) It adds the element successfully
B) It throws UnsupportedOperationException because List.of returns an immutable list
C) It throws compilation error
D) It creates a new copy automatically

**22. Explain JVM memory model and what volatile guarantees in Java 21+.**

**23. How do Virtual Threads (Project Loom, Java 21) differ from Platform Threads, and what is a pinning issue?**

**24. What is the difference between CompletableFuture and Structured Concurrency (Java 21 preview, Java 25 finalizing)?**

**25. Analyze this code for memory leak / resource leak:**
public class Cache {

Where Key doesn't override hashCode/equals and is mutable.

**27. How does synchronized work on the JVM and what are modern alternatives in Java 21+?**

C) synchronized uses CAS only
D) Virtual threads cannot use synchronized
**28. What is the difference between invokedynamic, invokestatic, invokevirtual, invokespecial, and invokeinterface in

A) All are same

--- PAGE 111 ---
B) invokestatic: static methods, invokespecial: private/constructor/super, invokevirtual: virtual dispatch for classes,
invokeinterface: interface dispatch. invokedynamic: dynamic call site resolution via BootstrapMethod, used for lambdas
(LambdaMetafactory), string concatenation (Java 9+), records; enables runtime linkage and performance
C) invokedynamic is only for Groovy

**29. Explain GC choices in Java 21-24 (G1, ZGC, Shenandoah, Generational ZGC) and when to use each.**
A) Only G1 exists
B) G1 (default): balanced low pause ~<200ms, good throughput, regions. ZGC (Java 21+ generational from Java 21/22):
sub-ms pauses, colored pointers, great for huge heaps (>100GB). Shenandoah: low-pause concurrent compacting.
Generational ZGC (Java 23): improves throughput by separating young/old. Choose G1 for general, ZGC/Shenandoah for
latency-sensitive large heaps
C) ZGC has higher pauses than G1
D) GC choice doesn't matter
**30. How do you correctly implement equals() and hashCode() contract for a class used in HashMap/HashSet in Java 17+?**
A) Only override equals
B) Override both: equals must be reflexive, symmetric, transitive, consistent, non-null false; hashCode must return same
value for equal objects; in Java 17+ use Objects.equals() and Objects.hash() or record (auto-implements), ensure
immutability of fields used, and instanceof pattern matching
C) Use == in equals
D) Return random hashCode
Answer Keys
**Beginner - Answer Key**
1. **Answer: B** - JVM is the virtual machine executing bytecode, JRE bundles JVM + core libs to run Java apps, JDK adds
javac and tools. This distinction was simplified since Java 11 where JRE distribution is not separate from JDK.
2. **Answer: B** - JVM looks for exactly public static void main(String[] args) (or varargs). Non-static fails to run, case matters
for Main.
3. **Answer: B** - == compares references. Literal goes to string pool, new String() creates heap object, so different refs. Use
.equals() for content.
4. **Answer: B** - Java has fixed sizes: byte 1, short 2, char 2 (UTF-16), int 4, long 8, float 4, double 8. boolean is virtual and
size is JVM dependent.
5. **Answer: A** - final variable assign once, final method cannot be overridden (but can be overloaded), final class cannot be
extended e.g. String.
6. **Answer: B** - Instance fields get defaults (0, null, false), locals do not. Primitive int defaults to 0, wrapper Integer (object)
defaults to null.
7. **Answer: B** - == for objects checks identity, .equals() is meant for logical equality and must be overridden. For String
literals due to interning == may appear true but unreliable.
8. **Answer: C** - protected: same package + subclasses even in other packages. Default is only same package, private only
same class.
9. **Answer: B** - Since Java 9, List.of, Set.of, Map.of produce unmodifiable immutable collections. Mutating methods throw
UnsupportedOperationException.
10. **Answer: B** - Overloading is compile-time polymorphism with same name different params. Overriding is runtime
polymorphism with same signature in subclass.
**Intermediate - Answer Key**
11. **Answer: B** - Record is compact immutable data carrier (Java 16+). Compiler synthesizes final fields, canonical
constructor, accessor methods named same as components, plus correct equals/hashCode/toString based on components.
12. **Answer: B** - newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor() creates new virtual thread per task, isVirtual() returns true for virtual
threads (Java 21 API). Virtual threads are cheap and ideal for blocking I/O.
13. **Answer: B** - Sealed classes (Java 17) restrict inheritance via permits. Permitted subclasses must be in same
module/package and declared final, sealed, or non-sealed to control continuation of sealing.

--- PAGE 112 ---
14. **Answer: B** - Pattern variable s is introduced after instanceof check and only in scope where compiler knows check
succeeded, including && RHS and true-branch. Reduces explicit casting boilerplate.
15. **Answer: B** - ArrayList contiguous array = fast index, slow middle insert. LinkedList nodes = slow index O(n), fast
Deque ops addFirst/last O(1). LinkedList rarely useful today; ArrayList + ArrayDeque preferred.
16. **Answer: B** - count++ is three ops: getfield, iadd, putfield, not atomic. Volatile fixes visibility but not atomicity. Use
AtomicInteger, synchronized, or VarHandle.
17. **Answer: B** - WeakHashMap wraps keys in WeakReference; when GC finds key only weakly reachable, entry is
expelled from map. Perfect for listener/metadata caches to avoid memory leaks.
18. **Answer: B** - If you map T -> Optional<U>, map gives Optional<Optional<U>>. flatMap flattens by expecting the
mapper to return Optional and not rewrap. Use flatMap to chain Optional pipelines.
19. **Answer: C** - SequencedCollection (Java 21) adds addFirst, addLast, getFirst, getLast, reversed(). After adding 0 at
front and 4 at back, first=0 last=4 sum=4.
20. **Answer: B** - transient tells ObjectOutputStream to skip serialization of field. try-with-resources (Java 7+) calls
AutoCloseable.close() automatically even on exception, suppressing secondary exceptions.
**Advanced - Answer Key**
21. **Answer: B** - Record patterns and pattern matching for switch became final in Java 21 (JEP 441). Compiler checks
sealed exhaustiveness, so no default required if all permits covered. Enables algebraic data types style.
22. **Answer: B** - Volatile reads/writes have memory semantics: visibility to all threads and prevents instr reordering around
volatile access. It does not make i++ atomic because it's compound; need atomic class or lock for RMW.
23. **Answer: B** - Virtual threads are JVM-managed, not OS threads, parked when blocking. Pinning occurs when virtual
thread is inside synchronized block or native call, the carrier platform thread cannot be released, reducing scalability. Fix by
using ReentrantLock.
24. **Answer: B** - CompletableFuture leads to callback hell if overused, manual cancellation. StructuredTaskScope (JEP
453/480) provides structured concurrency: fork subtasks in scope, join together, any failure cancels siblings, lifetime bound to
scope, ideal with virtual threads.
25. **Answer: B** - Static map never cleared = memory leak, strong refs prevent GC. Mutable keys breaking hashCode cause
bucket mismatch and duplicate entries. Use immutable keys with correct equals/hashCode, WeakHashMap/SoftReferences,
or bounded LRU cache.
26. **Answer: B** - JPMS modules declare module { exports ...; requires ... }. Encapsulation forbids split packages across
named modules because same package would have two sources. Migration fix: merge, re-package, or use automatic
modules.
27. **Answer: B** - Synchronized enters monitor via CAS and inflates to OS mutex under contention. Biased locking was
removed (JEP 374). For virtual threads prefer java.util.concurrent.locks to avoid pinning; synchronized will be updated in
future Loom to be virtual-thread friendly but still limited in 21-24.
28. **Answer: B** - invoke* are bytecode instructions for method dispatch. invokedynamic's bootstrap (e.g.,
LambdaMetafactory) returns CallSite; dynamic linkage allows lambdas to be spun via method handles not anonymous
classes, and string concat via StringConcatFactory, improving performance and future evolution.
29. **Answer: B** - G1 default, region-based with pause target. ZGC uses load barriers + colored pointers for <1ms pauses,
now generational by default in Java 23+ to improve throughput. Shenandoah Brooks pointers similar goals. Choose based on
latency vs throughput and heap size.
30. **Answer: B** - Contract: equal objects same hashCode, unequal may collide but should distribute. Use immutable fields,
Objects.equals for null-safe, Objects.hash. Records give correct implementation free. Always override both together or
neither.

--- PAGE 113 ---

Course: JavaScript
1. Beginner Level (10 Questions)
**Q1. What is the difference between var, let, and const in modern JavaScript (ES6+)?**
A. No difference, all are scoped the same
B. var is function-scoped and hoisted with initialization to undefined, let and const are block-scoped, hoisted but in Temporal
Dead Zone, and const requires initialization and prevents reassignment
C. let is function-scoped, var is block-scoped
D. const makes objects immutable deeply
**Q2. What will console.log(typeof null) output and what is null == undefined vs null === undefined?**
A. "null", true and true
B. "object", true and false
C. "undefined", false and false
D. "object", false and true
**Q3. What is the result of [] == ![] and why?**
A. true, because ![] is false, then [] == false coerces to 0 == 0
B. false, because array never equals boolean
C. true, because both are empty
D. TypeError
**Q4. Which method correctly adds an element to the end of an array and what does it return?**
A. arr.push(item) returns new length, arr.pop() removes last

C. arr.push(item) returns added item
D. arr.unshift(item) adds to end
**Q5. How do you correctly select an element with id app and add a click listener?**

B. document.querySelector('#app').addEventListener('click', handler)

D. document.querySelectorAll('app').addEventListener('click', handler)
**Q6. What does strict equality === check versus loose equality ==?**
A. === checks value only, == checks value and type
B. === checks value and type without coercion, == does type coercion before comparison
C. Both are identical in ES2024
D. == is faster than ===
**Q7. What will this log? for (var i=0; i<3; i++) { setTimeout(()=>console.log(i),0) } vs using let?**
A. 0,1,2 in both cases
B. var: 3,3,3 because var is shared closure; let: 0,1,2 because let creates per-iteration binding
C. TypeError
D. var: 0,1,2 let: 3,3,3
**Q8. Which statement about NaN is correct?**
A. NaN === NaN is true
B. NaN === NaN is false, use Number.isNaN(x) or Object.is(x, NaN) to check

--- PAGE 114 ---
C. typeof NaN is "NaN"
D. NaN == null is true
**Q9. What is the difference between a function declaration and a function expression in hoisting?**
A. function foo(){} is hoisted fully and callable before definition, const foo = function(){} is not, due to TDZ
B. Both are hoisted identically
C. Function expressions are hoisted, declarations are not
D. Arrow functions are hoisted
**Q10. What does JSON.stringify({a: 1, b: undefined, c: () => {}}) return?**
A. {"a":1,"b":null,"c":null}
B. {"a":1} because undefined and functions are omitted in objects, and become null in arrays
C. Error
D. {"a":1,"b":undefined}
Beginner Answer Key & Explanations
**Q1 Answer: B - var is function-scoped and hoisted with initialization to undefined, let/const block-scoped with TDZ**
var hoisting leads to bugs and allows redeclaration; let/const introduced block scope and TDZ to prevent access before
declaration. const only prevents reassignment of binding, not deep immutability.
**Q2 Answer: B - "object", true and false**
typeof null is "object" due to legacy bug from first JS implementation kept for compatibility. null == undefined is true by spec's
loose equality rules, but === is false because types differ.
**Q3 Answer: A - true, because coercion makes it 0 == 0**
![] coerces array to boolean true then negates to false. Then [] == false triggers ToNumber: [].toString() is "" -> 0, false -> 0, so
0 == 0 true.
**Q4 Answer: A - arr.push returns new length**
push() mutates array and returns new length for chaining length checks. unshift() adds to start, pop() returns removed
element.
**Q5 Answer: B - querySelector('#app').addEventListener**
querySelector with #id selector and addEventListener is standard modern DOM API supporting multiple listeners and options.
onClick property name is lowercase onclick and only allows one handler.
**Q6 Answer: B - === checks value and type without coercion, == does coercion**
Using === avoids surprising coercion rules like "0" == false being true. Linters in 2024-2026 enforce === by default via eslint
eqeqeq.
**Q7 Answer: B - var: 3,3,3 let: 0,1,2**
With var, there is one shared i binding captured by closure, so when timeout runs, loop finished at 3. ES6 let in for-loop spec
creates fresh binding per iteration.
**Q8 Answer: B - NaN !== NaN, use Number.isNaN**
IEEE 754 defines NaN not equal to itself. Number.isNaN does not coerce unlike global isNaN(), making it safe.
**Q9 Answer: A - Declaration hoisted fully, expression not**
Function declarations are hoisted with body, so callable before appearing in source. const foo = () => {} lives in TDZ until
initialization line executes.
**Q10 Answer: B - {"a":1} because undefined/functions omitted**
JSON.stringify spec omits undefined, functions, and Symbols from objects, and replaces them with null inside arrays. This
prevents invalid JSON.

**Q20. What is the difference between ES Modules import vs CommonJS require in 2024-2026 Node.js?**

Intermediate Answer Key & Explanations
**Q11 Answer: B - Closure retains private n per makeCounter call**
makeCounter creates execution context with n; inner arrow keeps lexical environment reference, preventing GC. Each call to
factory gets independent closure state.
**Q12 Answer: A - 1,5,2,3,4**
Sync code 1,5 runs first. Microtasks FIFO: Promise.then queued first, then queueMicrotask. Both run before next macrotask
setTimeout(0), which is event loop spec.
**Q13 Answer: A - Arrow lexical this, regular dynamic this**
Arrow captures this at definition time for callbacks; regular function's this determined at call site, allowing .call(). This is why
arrow unsuitable as object method or constructor.
**Q14 Answer: A - Both return Promise resolving to 1, return await adds extra tick and error handling semantics**
async always wraps return in Promise.resolve. return await unwraps and re-wraps, preserving async stack traces in V8 and
allowing surrounding try/catch to catch the rejection from awaited promise.
**Q15 Answer: A - a=1,b=3,rest=[4,5], y=10**
Comma elision skips index 1, rest collects remaining. Nested pattern {x:{y}} extracts deep property; fails if x undefined unless
default provided.
**Q16 Answer: A - fetch only rejects on network failure**
Fetch spec treats HTTP statuses as successful transport; ok is true for 200-299. You must manually if (!r.ok) throw new
Error(r.status).
**Q17 Answer: B - class is sugar over prototypes; getPrototypeOf preferred**
JS remains prototype-based; class defines prototype methods non-enumerable. __proto__ is legacy accessor;
Object.create(null) avoids prototype pollution for safe maps.
**Q18 Answer: B - Clear timeout and set new**
Debounce ensures fn called once after quiet period; clearTimeout cancels prior schedule. Throttle is different: limits to at most
once per interval.
**Q19 Answer: A - all fails fast, allSettled waits, any first fulfilled, plus Object.groupBy ES2024**
Promise.all short-circuits on rejection, useful for dependent tasks. ES2024 finalized Object.groupBy(array, callback) returning
plain object, and Map.groupBy.
**Q20 Answer: A - ESM static live bindings vs CJS dynamic sync**
ESM imports are hoisted and static-analyzable for tree-shaking; Node requires explicit .js extension in ESM. Interop: you can
import() CJS from ESM, but require() ESM throws ERR_REQUIRE_ESM since Node 18+.

**Q28. How does AbortController work with fetch and what new ES2024 pattern combines multiple signals via

--- PAGE 118 ---
A. const ac=new AbortController(); fetch(url,{signal:ac.signal}) abort triggers AbortError DOMException;
AbortSignal.any([signal1, timeoutSignal]) composes signals; AbortSignal.timeout(5000) auto-aborts
B. AbortController not usable with fetch
C. any() does OR but doesn't abort
D. fetch abort returns null
**Q29. What is memory leak risk with closures + event listeners + timers, and how to detect? Example: SPA adds
window.addEventListener('resize', () => hugeArray) without cleanup?**
A. No leak possible in JS
B. Listener retains closure referencing hugeArray and prevents GC even after component unmount; timer retains scope; leaks
visible in Chrome DevTools Memory heap snapshot -> Detached DOM, and Performance monitor; fix with
removeEventListener, WeakRef for non-essential, or AbortSignal for listeners addEventListener(name, fn, {signal})
C. GC always cleans listeners
D. WeakRef fixes all leaks automatically
**Q30. Predict output and explain temporal: console.log(0); async function a(){ console.log(1); await null; console.log(2); } a();
console.log(3); Promise.resolve().then(()=>console.log(4)); plus Temporal API status 2024-2026?**
A. 0,1,3,2,4? Actually await null yields microtask: 0,1,3,2,4 or 0,1,3,4,2 depending on enqueue order; await always defers
continuation as microtask. Temporal API (Temporal.Now, Temporal.PlainDate) is Stage 3 proposal for immutable date
handling to replace Date, available behind flag/polyfill, expected 2025-2026 final
B. 0,1,2,3,4 sync
C. 0,3,1,2,4
D. 0,1,3,4,2 with Temporal already in browsers globally by 2024
Advanced Answer Key & Explanations
**Q21 Answer: A - Map strong ref prevents GC and is iterable, WeakMap weak ref allows GC and not iterable**
WeakMap keys must be objects and held weakly to avoid memory leaks for caches attached to DOM nodes. Non-iterability
prevents exposing GC non-determinism, which is spec requirement.
**Q22 Answer: B - Standardized {promise, resolve, reject} without executor scope hack**
ES2024 added Promise.withResolvers() to avoid capturing resolver via closure inside executor and to simplify external
resolution patterns. It returns sealed object with explicit resolve/reject for interop with callback APIs.
**Q23 Answer: A - Infinite microtasks starve macrotask**
Event loop algorithm: drain microtask queue fully before running next macrotask (timer, I/O, render). Recursive
queueMicrotask prevents loop progressing to timers/render, freezing UI; detection via Long Task.
**Q24 Answer: B - Proxy must respect invariants**
Proxy invariants ensure observable behavior remains consistent with target non-configurable properties, protecting engine
optimizations and security assumptions. Violation throws TypeError rather than silently returning wrong value.
**Q25 Answer: B - Consistent order retains HiddenClass, delete forces dictionary mode**
V8 creates hidden classes (Maps) for objects sharing property layout for fast offset access via inline caches. Out-of-order or
delete transitions to dictionary (hash table) slow path, 10-100x slower property load.
**Q26 Answer: B - Non-mutating copying methods + groupBy**
ES2023 introduced toSorted/toReversed/toSpliced/with for immutable patterns needed by React/Redux avoiding
copy-then-mutate. ES2024 Object.groupBy returns {key: [items]} and Map.groupBy for non-string keys.
**Q27 Answer: B - eval and innerHTML dangerous, use textContent, Trusted Types, DOMPurify**
XSS occurs because input is parsed as code/HTML. Modern defense: textContent for text, Trusted Types CSP
(require-trusted-types-for 'script') blocks innerHTML assignment of strings forcing policy, plus sanitize libraries.
**Q28 Answer: A - AbortController signal triggers AbortError, AbortSignal.any and timeout are ES2024**
AbortController is standard cancellation token; fetch checks signal and rejects with name:'AbortError'. ES2024 added
AbortSignal.any(iterable) which aborts when any source aborts, and timeout(ms) factory for deadlines.
**Q29 Answer: B - Listener retains closure prevents GC, use remove + AbortSignal**

--- PAGE 119 ---
Detached DOM + large closures are classic SPA leaks. Chrome heap snapshot shows retained size chain. Using {signal:
AbortSignal} with ac.abort() to bulk remove listeners on unmount, plus clearing timers, is 2024 best practice.
**Q30 Answer: D - 0,1,3,4,2 with Temporal proposal Stage 3**
Order: sync 0, enters async a logs 1, await null -> Promise.resolve(null).then(continuation) queues microtask. Sync logs 3.
Microtasks FIFO: then(()=>4) queued before await continuation only if promise already resolved? Actually await null wraps via
Promise.resolve, so 4 queued before 2 if a() called before Promise.resolve().then; spec yields 0,1,3,4,2. Temporal API
provides correct dates without Date mutability, currently polyfilled.

--- PAGE 120 ---
Course File: 18_TypeScript.md
# TypeScript - Professional Certification Exam
**Total Questions:** 30 | **Duration:** 90 minutes | **Passing Score:** 70%
Section A: Beginner (Questions 1-10)
Q1. What command compiles a TypeScript file app.ts to JavaScript?
A) typescript app.ts
B) tsc app.ts
C) ts-node compile app.ts
D) npm run app.ts
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** tsc is the official TypeScript Compiler CLI. It transpiles .ts files to JavaScript according to tsconfig.json or
default settings, producing app.js.
Q2. Which type annotation correctly declares a variable that can be a
string or null?
A) let x: string & null
B) let x: string | null
C) let x: string || null
D) let x: nullable<string>
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Union types use the | operator to allow multiple types. string | null means the value can be either a string or
null, which is required under strictNullChecks.
Q3. What is the default type of a variable declared without initialization and
without annotation under noImplicitAny: true?
A) unknown
B) any but with an error
C) never
D) void
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** With noImplicitAny enabled, let x; causes a compiler error for implicit any. The compiler infers any but forces
you to explicitly annotate to prevent unsafe typing.
Q4. Which keyword defines a custom type alias in TypeScript?
A) interface
B) type
C) alias
D) def
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** The type keyword creates a type alias, e.g., type UserID = string | number. Unlike interface, it can alias
primitives, unions, tuples, and mapped types.

--- PAGE 121 ---
Q5. What does the ? after a property name mean in an interface?
A) The property is private
B) The property is optional and may be undefined
C) The property is readonly
D) The property is deprecated
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Optional properties are marked with ?, like age?: number. This is shorthand for age: number | undefined,
meaning the property can be omitted when creating the object.
Q6. Which array type syntax is correct for an array of numbers?
A) let arr: number[] and let arr: Array<number>
B) let arr: [number]
C) let arr: Number[]
D) let arr: numbers
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** Both number[] and Array<number> are valid and equivalent syntax for number arrays. [number] denotes a
tuple with exactly one element, not an open-ended array.
Q7. What does void mean as a function return type?
A) The function returns null
B) The function returns no value / undefined
C) The function never returns
D) The function returns any type
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** void indicates a function doesn't return a meaningful value, typically returning undefined. Use never instead
when a function never returns at all, such as throwing or infinite loops.
Q8. How do you enable strict type checking in tsconfig.json?
A) "strict": true
B) "strictMode": true
C) "typeCheck": "strict"
D) "noAny": true
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** Setting "strict": true enables all strict family checks including strictNullChecks, noImplicitAny,
strictFunctionTypes, etc. This is the recommended setting for all new projects since TS 2.3.
Q9. Which is the correct way to type a function parameter with a default
value?
A) function greet(name?: string = "Guest") {}
B) function greet(name: string = "Guest") {}
C) function greet(name = "Guest": string) {}
D) function greet(default "Guest": string) {}
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** In function greet(name: string = "Guest"), TypeScript infers the type from the default, so : string is actually
optional. The optional ? is not needed because a default value implicitly makes the parameter optional.

--- PAGE 122 ---
Q10. What does as do in const x = someValue as string?
A) Declares a new variable
B) Performs runtime conversion to string
C) Type assertion telling compiler to treat value as string
D) Imports a string module
**Answer:** C
**Explanation:** as is a type assertion that overrides the compiler's inferred type without runtime effect. It does not convert the
value; as is purely compile-time, unlike a cast in C# or Java.
Section B: Intermediate (Questions 11-20)
Q11. What is the difference between interface and type for object shapes in
2024+ TypeScript?
A) They are identical in all cases
B) interface supports declaration merging and is generally preferred for public API object shapes, while type can express
unions, intersections, and mapped types that interface cannot
C) type supports declaration merging
D) interface cannot be extended
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Interfaces merge automatically and offer better error messages and extends clause for objects, which is why
libraries prefer them. Type aliases are more powerful for unions (A | B), primitives, and utility type compositions.
Q12. Given type A = {x: number} & {y: string}, what is A?
A) Union type
B) Intersection type requiring both x and y
C) Invalid syntax
D) Overload
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** The & operator creates an intersection, combining members of both types. An object of type A must have
both x: number and y: string; overlapping properties must be compatible.
Q13. What does the unknown type force you to do before using a value?
A) Nothing, same as any
B) Perform type narrowing / checking before operations
C) Convert to any first
D) Use await
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** unknown is the type-safe counterpart to any; you cannot access properties or call it without narrowing. You
must use typeof, instanceof, or custom type guards to refine it to a usable type.
Q14. Which generic constraint ensures T has a length property?
A) <T extends { length: number }>
B) <T where length>
C) <T has length>

--- PAGE 123 ---
D) <T: {length}>
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** extends in generics constrains the type parameter to satisfy a shape. <T extends { length: number }> allows
using arg.length inside the function while still preserving the specific type T.
Q15. What is a discriminated union?
A) A union with a common literal property used to narrow the type
B) A union of only primitive types
C) A union that TypeScript cannot discriminate
D) An enum
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** A discriminated union has a common property like kind or type with literal types, e.g., {kind: "circle", r:
number} | {kind: "square", size: number}. TypeScript can narrow via switch (shape.kind) from TS 4.4+ with improved control
flow analysis.
Q16. What does keyof do?
A) Returns array of keys at runtime
B) Produces a union of string literal types of the keys of a type
C) Checks if key exists
D) Creates an enum of keys
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** keyof Person for Person = {name: string, age: number} yields "name" | "age". It operates purely at the type
level and is heavily used with Mapped Types like keyof T paired with T[K].
Q17. What will typeof do in a type context like type T = typeof myVar?
A) Returns runtime string "string"/"number" etc.
B) Captures the TypeScript type of myVar as a type
C) Causes error
D) Returns any
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** In type position, typeof is a type query that extracts the static type of a variable or property. This enables
patterns like type Config = typeof defaultConfig without duplicating the shape.
Q18. Which utility type makes all properties of T optional?
A) Required<T>
B) Partial<T>
C) Pick<T>
D) Optional<T>
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Partial<T> maps to { [P in keyof T]?: T[P] }. It's built into lib since TS 2.1 and essential for update patterns;
Required<T> does the opposite, removing ?.
Q19. What does the ! non-null assertion operator do?
A) Makes value null
B) Asserts that a value is non-null/non-undefined, removing null/undefined from type

--- PAGE 124 ---
C) Throws if null
D) Converts to boolean
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** value! tells the compiler that you know value is not nullish despite its type allowing it. It's unsafe and should
be sparing; unlike runtime checks, it emits no JS code and can cause runtime errors if wrong.
Q20. How do you correctly type an async function returning a number?
A) async function get(): number {}
B) async function get(): Promise<number> {}
C) async function get(): Async<number> {}
D) function get(): async number {}
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Async functions always return a Promise wrapping the declared return. So Promise<number> is correct;
TypeScript will error if you annotate just number as the return type of an async function.
Section C: Advanced (Questions 21-30)
Q21. What does the satisfies operator introduced in TypeScript 4.9 do?
A) Same as as
B) Validates that an expression satisfies a type without widening or changing its inferred type
C) Checks runtime satisfaction
D) Deprecates type assertion
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** const palette = {red: [255,0,0]} satisfies Record<string, RGB> ensures compatibility while keeping literal tuple
types precise. Unlike as, it preserves the narrowest inferred type for later intellisense and errors.
Q22. Given type A = ReturnType<typeof fn>, what does ReturnType use
under the hood?
A) Conditional type with infer
B) keyof and lookup
C) typeof only
D) Decorator metadata
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** ReturnType<T> is defined as T extends (...args: any[]) => infer R ? R : never. The infer R keyword inside
conditional types extracts the return type, a core pattern for advanced type transformations.
Q23. What is the difference between in and out variance annotations on
type parameters (TS 4.7+)?
A) They are aliases for extends
B) in marks contravariant (only written), out marks covariant (only read); they enable stricter checking of generic variance
C) in means input file, out means output file
D) No difference
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** Variance annotations improve type safety for generics like type Getter<out T> and type Setter<in T>. They
were introduced in TS 4.7 to allow the compiler to correctly check assignability based on usage direction, crucial for library

--- PAGE 125 ---
authors.
Q24. Consider: type IsNever<T> = [T] extends [never] ? true : false. Why
wrap in tuple [T]?
A) To avoid distributive conditional type behavior
B) For performance
C) Syntax requirement
D) To make it mutable
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** Naked T extends ... distributes over unions, and never is the empty union, so never extends ... would be
skipped entirely. Wrapping in [T] disables distribution, allowing correct detection of never.
Q25. What is a template literal type example introduced in TS 4.1?
A) type Event = on${Capitalize<string>}
B) type Event = "on" + string
C) type Event = string.template()
D) type Event = String.Template
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** Template literal types allow type Greeting = Hello ${string} and with intrinsic manipulators like Capitalize,
Uppercase, Lowercase, Uncapitalize. They power typed event emitters like ${key}Changed from object keys.
Q26. With moduleResolution: "bundler" and verbatimModuleSyntax (TS
5.0+), what is required?
A) All imports must be type-only if they are types
B) type imports must use import type syntax, and verbatimModuleSyntax preserves import statements exactly as written
without elision
C) No changes
D) CommonJS must be used
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** verbatimModuleSyntax (replacing importsNotUsedAsValues and preserveValueImports) forces explicit
import type { Foo } for type-only imports. This aligns TS with bundlers like Vite/esbuild under "bundler" resolution, preventing
runtime import errors from erased types.
Q27. What does const enum become at compile time vs enum?
A) Same as regular enum
B) const enum is fully inlined at use sites with no object emitted (unless preserveConstEnums), while regular enum emits a
real object
C) const enum emits more code
D) const enum is deprecated
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** const enum Color { Red=0 } with Color.Red usage compiles directly to 0, enabling zero-cost abstraction.
However, const enum cannot be used across isolatedModules without isolatedDeclarations constraints and is discouraged in
libraries for compatibility.
Q28. What is the noUncheckedIndexedAccess flag (relevant for TS 5.5+
Maps/Sets)?

--- PAGE 126 ---
A) Adds | undefined to indexed access types like array[i] and obj[key] where key is not a known property
B) Disables indexing
C) Makes all properties readonly
D) Checks array bounds at runtime
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** When enabled, obj[key] or arr[0] becomes T | undefined even if T[] is declared, forcing safety checks. Since
TS 5.5 this flag's behavior is widely recommended and aligns with safer indexing, especially for dynamic key access.
Q29. Explain infer in type UnwrapPromise<T> = T extends Promise<infer
U> ? U : T
A) infer U declares a new type variable to capture inner Promise type
B) infer is runtime promise unwrapping
C) It throws if not promise
D) It converts T to unknown
**Answer:** A
**Explanation:** infer introduces a placeholder type variable within the extends clause of a conditional type. In Promise<infer
U>, TS infers what U would need to be for T to match Promise<U>, effectively unwrapping it.
Q30. How does using and Disposable / Symbol.dispose work in TypeScript
5.2+?
A) It's a comment
B) using declares an explicit resource that auto-disposes at scope exit via [Symbol.dispose](), modeled after TC39 Explicit
Resource Management proposal, with await using for async disposal
C) It deletes variables
D) It imports disposal module
**Answer:** B
**Explanation:** using file = new FileHandle() will call file[Symbol.dispose]() when the block exits, like C# using or Python
context managers. TypeScript 5.2 adds type support for Disposable and AsyncDisposable, emitting try/finally blocks when
targeting below ES23.

**Q1 Answer: A**
> / always returns float, // is floor division, % is remainder. So 5/2=2.5, 5//2=2, 5%2=1.
**Q2 Answer: B**
> ** is exponentiation, ^ is bitwise XOR in Python. Comprehension [x**2 for x in range(5)] produces [0,1,4,9,16].
**Q3 Answer: B**
> strip(), lstrip(), rstrip() remove leading/trailing whitespace by default, or specified characters. They don't affect interior
characters.
**Q4 Answer: B**
> Duplicate keys overwrite previous values; {"a": 1, "b": 2, "a": 3} keeps only last "a" so dict has 2 entries. Length is therefore
2.
**Q5 Answer: B**
> Python uses try: block followed by except ExceptionType: and optionally else and finally. Other languages use catch/throw.
**Q6 Answer: B**
> == invokes __eq__ for value equality, is compares identity via id(). Use is for None checks, == for values.
**Q7 Answer: B**
> The with statement uses context manager protocol (__enter__/__exit__) to guarantee close even on exceptions. It's the
idiomatic safe pattern.
**Q8 Answer: B**
> Empty collections, empty string, 0, 0.0, None, and False are falsy in Python's truth testing. All three examples evaluate to
False.
**Q9 Answer: B**
> Non-default parameters must precede default parameters. def func(a, b=2): follows this rule; others put default before
non-default which is SyntaxError.
**Q10 Answer: B**
> range(start, stop, step) stops before stop value. Starting at 2, stepping by 2 until 10 gives 2,4,6,8.
Intermediate
**Q11 Answer: B**
> make_adder returns a closure capturing n=5. Calling add5(10) executes lambda 10+5, returning 15. Closures retain
enclosing scope.
**Q12 Answer: B**
> PEP 701 (Python 3.12) rewrote f-string parsing using the PEG parser: nested f-strings, multi-line, quotes reuse, comments
and backslashes inside now allowed.

--- PAGE 134 ---
**Q13 Answer: B**
> MRO is computed by C3 linearization and viewable via Class.__mro__. It resolves which parent method is called in
diamond inheritance.
**Q14 Answer: B**
> b = a is reference alias, so b.append mutates a. c = a.copy() was shallow copy before append, so c remains [1,2,3].
**Q15 Answer: A**
> @property turns method into descriptor for attribute access, enabling computed attributes with @x.setter. It manages
getter/setter logic transparently.
**Q16 Answer: A**
> PEP 695 in Python 3.12 introduced type parameter syntax: class Stack[T]: and def func[T](x: T) -> T:. No import of TypeVar
needed.
**Q17 Answer: B**
> __name__ is "__main__" when run directly, module name when imported. Idiom prevents side effects on import and
enables script/module dual use.
**Q18 Answer: A**
> *args packs extra positional args into tuple, **kwargs packs extra keyword args into dict. Useful for decorators and
forwarding calls.
**Q19 Answer: B**
> PEP 703 free-threaded build (no GIL) landed experimentally in 3.13t, installed via python3.13t, enabled with ./configure
--disable-gil. Check via sys._is_gil_enabled().
**Q20 Answer: C**
> Match case case 500 | 502 | 503: is OR pattern matching. Code 502 matches that branch returning "Server Error".
Introduced in PEP 634.
Advanced
**Q21 Answer: B**
> Walrus operator := assigns expression value to variable as part of larger expression and returns value. n becomes 6 and
remains accessible in current scope.
**Q22 Answer: B**
> gather(*coros) schedules coroutines concurrently on event loop, awaits all, returns results in input order. It's not threading
but cooperative concurrency.
**Q23 Answer: A**
> PEP 654 introduced ExceptionGroup and except* (Python 3.11). except* can partially handle a group, extracting matching
subtypes while leaving others.
**Q24 Answer: B**
> __slots__ = ('x','y') prevents __dict__ creation, saving ~50% memory per instance and faster attribute access. Attempts to
set unlisted attrs raises AttributeError.
**Q25 Answer: B**
> Descriptor protocol __get__/__set__ intercepts attribute access when defined on class. a.x = "hi" calls Typed.__set__,
which raises TypeError if not int.
**Q26 Answer: A**
> PEP 695 adds type statement for type aliases: type Vector[T] = list[T] creates generic alias. This replaces TypeAlias
assignment style.
**Q27 Answer: B**
> Shallow copy copies outer list only, inner lists shared. Mutating b[0][0] mutates a[0][0]. Deepcopy recursively copies, so c
unaffected. Prints 99 1.
**Q28 Answer: B**

--- PAGE 135 ---
> TaskGroup (PEP 654, Python 3.11) implements structured concurrency: if one child fails, others cancelled; all exceptions
combined into ExceptionGroup; context exit waits.
**Q29 Answer: A**
> Metaclass is class of a class; type(name, bases, dict) constructs classes. Custom metaclass overrides __new__/__init__ to
customize class creation, used for ORM, validation frameworks.
**Q30 Answer: A**
> Python 3.13 ships experimental JIT (PEP 744 copy-and-patch) buildable with --enable-experimental-jit and --disable-gil
free-threaded. Both are opt-in experiments, not default, with other perf wins.
**Scoring Guide**
- Beginner (Q1-Q10): 3 pts each = 30 pts
- Intermediate (Q11-Q20): 3 pts each = 30 pts
- Advanced (Q21-Q30): 4 pts each = 40 pts
- Total: 100 pts | Passing 70%
