# PHP & Laravel - Comprehensive Exam (30 Questions)
This exam covers PHP 8.1-8.4 and Laravel 10-12 (2024-2026 standards). Each question has 4 options with answer key and
2-sentence explanation.
Section 1: Beginner - 10 Questions
Q1. What does the PHP null coalescing operator ?? do?
- A) Checks if file exists
- B) Returns the first operand if it exists and is not null, otherwise returns second operand
- C) Merges two arrays
- D) Converts null to false
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The ?? operator checks if the left side is set and not null, returning it otherwise falling back. It is shorthand for
isset() ternary and prevents undefined variable notices.
Q2. In PHP 8+, what is the correct way to declare a typed property that can
be null?
- A) public ?string $name = null;
- B) public string nullable $name;
- C) public string $name = NULL;
- D) public null $name;
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Nullable types use ?Type prefix, and must have a default value if nullable in promoted properties context.
This enforces type safety while allowing null values.
Q3. Which Artisan command creates a new Laravel controller?
- A) php artisan make:control
- B) php artisan create:controller MyController
- C) php artisan make:controller MyController
- D) php artisan new:controller MyController
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** make:controller is the standard Artisan generator for controllers. It can take --resource or --api flags to
scaffold methods.
Q4. In Laravel, what is Eloquent?
- A) A JavaScript framework
- B) Laravel's ORM for database interactions using ActiveRecord pattern
- C) A caching driver
- D) A templating engine
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Eloquent is Laravel's Object-Relational Mapper that maps database tables to model classes. It provides
expressive syntax for queries, relationships, and mutators.
Q5. Which Blade directive checks authentication in Laravel?

--- PAGE 137 ---
- A) @auth and @guest
- B) @ifAuth
- C) @loggedIn
- D) @checkAuth
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** @auth renders content only for authenticated users, while @guest does the opposite for unauthenticated
users. They can take a guard parameter like @auth('admin').
Q6. What is Composer in PHP?
- A) A PHP code compiler
- B) A dependency manager for PHP
- C) A web server
- D) A database
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Composer manages PHP dependencies via composer.json and autoloading per PSR-4. It installs packages
from Packagist and handles version constraints.
Q7. How do you define a basic route in Laravel (Laravel 11+)?
- A) Route::get('/hello', function(){ return 'Hello'; }); in routes/web.php
- B) Router::define('/hello')
- C) Route::map('/hello')
- D) App::route('/hello')
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Routes are defined in routes/web.php or api.php using the Route facade. The closure or controller action is
executed when the URI matches.
Q8. What does echo vs print difference in PHP?
- A) echo can take multiple args and has no return, print returns 1 and takes single arg
- B) They are identical
- C) print is faster
- D) echo only works in Blade
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** echo is a language construct that can output multiple comma-separated values and returns void. print always
returns 1, making it usable in expressions but slightly slower.
Q9. What is the purpose of .env file in Laravel?
- A) To store frontend CSS
- B) To store environment-specific configuration like DB credentials and APP_KEY
- C) To store routes
- D) To store Blade views
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** .env holds sensitive environment variables loaded by vlucas/phpdotenv into $_ENV. Config files then access
them via env() helper, but should be cached via config() in production.
Q10. Which function starts a session in plain PHP and is handled
automatically by Laravel middleware?

--- PAGE 138 ---
- A) session_start()
- B) begin_session()
- C) start_session()
- D) init_session()
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** In vanilla PHP you must call session_start() before output. Laravel handles this automatically via
StartSession middleware in the web group, so you use session() helper instead.
Section 2: Intermediate - 10 Questions
Q11. What are PHP 8.1+ Enums and how are they used in Laravel
validation?
- A) Enums are not supported in PHP
- B) Native enumerated types: enum Status: string { case Published = 'published'; } and validated with
Rule::enum(Status::class)
- C) Enums are just constants
- D) Enums only work in JavaScript
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** PHP 8.1 introduced backed and pure Enums as first-class types. Laravel 9+ added Rule::enum() validation
rule and implicit enum casting in Eloquent models.
Q12. In Laravel 11, how is the application bootstrap configured differently?
- A) Still uses app/Http/Kernel.php for everything
- B) Uses bootstrap/app.php with Application::configure()->withRouting()->withMiddleware()->withExceptions()->create() and
no default Kernel files
- C) Uses config/app.php only
- D) Kernel files are required
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Laravel 11 introduced a slimmer skeleton removing Http Kernel and Console Kernel by default. All routing,
middleware, and exception handling is now configured fluently in bootstrap/app.php.
Q13. Explain Laravel Service Container and Dependency Injection.
- A) Service container only stores config
- B) The IoC container manages class dependencies and automatic resolution via type-hinting in constructors/methods
- C) It is a Docker container
- D) It is for frontend services
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The service container binds abstractions to concretions and auto-resolves dependencies via reflection. This
enables loose coupling, testability via mocking, and automatic injection in controllers, jobs, listeners.
Q14. What does readonly property and readonly class mean in PHP
8.2/8.3?
- A) Property can be written only once after initialization, and readonly class makes all properties readonly
- B) It makes class immutable permanently
- C) It is for constants only
- D) It prevents inheritance

--- PAGE 139 ---
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** readonly properties can only be initialized once and cannot be modified afterward, introduced in PHP 8.1 and
expanded to classes in 8.2. This is ideal for DTOs and Value Objects, commonly used with Laravel Data packages.
Q15. How do you handle N+1 query problem in Laravel Eloquent?
- A) Use with() for eager loading, e.g., Post::with('author')->get() and check with preventLazyLoading() or Telescope
- B) Use more foreach loops
- C) Disable Eloquent
- D) Use sleep()
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** N+1 occurs when lazy loading relationships inside loops causes extra queries. Eager loading with with()
loads relationships in 1-2 queries, and Laravel 11+ can enforce with Model::preventLazyLoading() in development.
Q16. What is the difference between hasOne vs belongsTo in Eloquent?
- A) They are same
- B) hasOne owns the foreign key on related model, belongsTo owns foreign key on current model
- C) belongsTo is for many-to-many
- D) hasOne is only for users
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In Post belongsTo User, posts table has user_id. In User hasOne Profile, profiles table has the foreign key.
The model that holds the FK uses belongsTo.
Q17. What is new in PHP 8.3 typed class constants and PHP 8.4 property
hooks?
- A) No new features in 8.3/8.4
- B) PHP 8.3 allows const string VERSION = '1.0'; typed constants, and PHP 8.4 introduces property hooks get/set and
asymmetric visibility public private(set)
- C) Typed constants were removed
- D) Property hooks are for Blade only
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** PHP 8.3 added typed class constants improving static analysis. PHP 8.4 adds property hooks for computed
accessors and asymmetric visibility allowing public get but private set, reducing boilerplate getters/setters.
Q18. How do Laravel Queues and Jobs work in 2024-2026 best practices?
- A) Queues run synchronously only
- B) Jobs are dispatched to a queue driver (redis, database, SQS) and processed by php artisan queue:work, with Laravel 11
using php artisan queue:work --sleep=3 and job batches, chains, and ShouldBeUnique
- C) Queues are for frontend
- D) Jobs cannot fail
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Queued jobs enable async heavy tasks like emails. Modern Laravel supports job middleware, unique jobs,
batching, chaining, and Horizon for Redis monitoring, with failed jobs stored in failed_jobs table.
Q19. What is Laravel's upsert() and updateOrInsert() distinction?
- A) Both identical

--- PAGE 140 ---
- B) updateOrInsert() checks single record and updates/inserts (boolean), upsert() does bulk insert with update on duplicate
key via unique index for high performance
- C) upsert deletes
- D) Only upsert exists
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** updateOrInsert is for single row with where conditions and is not bulk efficient. upsert leverages SQL
INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE for thousands of rows in one query, requiring unique constraint columns specified.
Q20. How does Laravel 11 handle API rate limiting and middleware
definition?
- A) Rate limiting removed
- B) RateLimiter::for('api', fn() => Limit::perMinute(60)) in AppServiceProvider and middleware aliased in bootstrap/app.php
via $middleware->alias(['role' => RoleMiddleware::class])
- C) Only .htaccess controls it
- D) Middleware defined in Kernel only
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Rate limiters are defined in service providers and referenced in routes via throttle:api. In Laravel 11, custom
middleware aliases are registered fluently in bootstrap/app.php instead of Http Kernel $routeMiddleware array.
Section 3: Advanced - 10 Questions
Q21. Explain PHP 8.2-8.4 Fibers and how they relate to Laravel Octane.
- A) Fibers are synchronous loops
- B) Fibers are lightweight cooperative concurrency primitives allowing non-blocking I/O pause/resume via Fiber::suspend();
Laravel Octane (Swoole/RoadRunner/FrankenPHP) uses them for concurrent tasks and Octane::concurrently()
- C) Fibers are database fibers
- D) Fibers replace queues
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Fibers enable async-like code without callback hell and are used internally by Laravel's concurrency
features. Octane keeps app in memory and uses Fibers to run multiple operations concurrently to reduce latency.
Q22. How does Laravel's new once() helper, defer(), and deferred functions
in Laravel 11/12 improve performance?
- A) They do not exist
- B) once(fn) memoizes result per request, Illuminate\Support\defer(fn) and defer() after response executes work after
sending response to user (terminable), similar to fastcgi_finish_request
- C) defer delays database
- D) once creates cookies
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** once() prevents repeated heavy computations within same request lifecycle. Deferred callbacks in Laravel
11+ run after response is sent, ideal for non-critical tasks without full queue overhead, improving TTFB.
Q23. Deep dive: How does Eloquent handle $casts, custom cast classes,
and AsEncryptedCollection in 2024?
- A) Casts only for dates
- B) $casts = ['price' => 'decimal:2', 'options' => AsCollection::class] + custom CastsAttributes classes + Laravel 11 encrypted
casts like encrypted:array, and attribute accessor via Attribute::make(get/set)

--- PAGE 141 ---
- C) Casts are deprecated
- D) Only string casts work
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Modern casts use classes implementing CastsAttributes or new Attribute accessor/mutator syntax. Laravel
10+ added AsEncryptedCollection, AsEncryptedArrayObject and improved decimal casting for money handling without
floating errors.
Q24. What is the correct way to prevent mass-assignment vulnerability and
how did Laravel 11 change $fillable vs $guarded best practice combined
with DTOs?
- A) Use $fillable whitelist of assignable fields or $guarded = [] with caution; use Validated DTO or $request->validated() never
$request->all() for create/update
- B) Use $_POST directly
- C) No need to guard
- D) Mass assignment is not a vulnerability
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Mass assignment allows attackers to inject unexpected fields like is_admin. Best practice is strict $fillable
plus using FormRequest validated() data and Spatie Laravel Data DTOs to ensure only validated fields pass.
Q25. Explain Laravel's new Eloquent where clauses, subquery
optimizations, and Cursor vs Lazy vs Chunk.
- A) All methods same
- B) chunk() loads fixed size with offset (issues with modifications), lazy() uses cursor with where id > last, cursor() uses PDO
cursor single query low memory but cannot count; advanced where like whereRelation, whereJsonContains, whereExists
subqueries
- C) Cursor is fastest always
- D) Lazy loads everything
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** chunk() paginates with LIMIT/OFFSET which can skip rows if data mutates. lazy() streams via chunked id
queries safe for large datasets. cursor() holds single DB cursor open, using PHP generators for lowest memory but needs
careful transaction handling.
Q26. How do you implement proper transaction, deadlock handling, and
pessimistic locking in Laravel for high concurrency (e.g., inventory)?
- A) Just save model
- B) Use DB::transaction(fn, attempts:3) with sharedLock() and lockForUpdate(), SELECT ... FOR UPDATE via
->lockForUpdate(), idempotency keys, and catch DeadlockException retry
- C) Transactions not needed
- D) Use sleep to prevent deadlocks
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** DB::transaction() auto-commits/rollbacks and can retry on deadlock by attempts param. Pessimistic locking
with lockForUpdate() prevents race conditions for stock decrement, essential for inventory/booking systems where optimistic
locking fails.
Q27. What are PHP 8.4 deprecations and Laravel 11/12 breaking changes
to watch for in 2026?
- A) Nothing deprecated

--- PAGE 142 ---
- B) PHP 8.4 deprecates implicit nullable function foo(Type $x = null) in favor of ?Type, deprecates passing E_NOTICE as
string; Laravel 11 removes app/Http/Middleware defaults, removes php artisan make:controller --model auto import changes,
PHPUnit 11 requires ReturnTypeWillChange fixes, Carbon 3 compatibility
- C) PHP deprecates classes
- D) Laravel removes Eloquent
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** PHP actively cleans implicit nullable type which emits deprecation in 8.4 and becomes error in 9.0. Laravel
11 slims defaults, so upgrading requires moving middleware to bootstrap/app.php and updating from Doctrine/dbal to native
schema methods.
Q28. How does Laravel Sanctum vs Passport vs new Laravel 12 Passport +
Socialite vs PASETO work for APIs?
- A) All same for tokens
- B) Sanctum SPA token + mobile personal access tokens (simple, no OAuth server), Passport full OAuth2 server with scopes
(heavier), Laravel Airlock history; for 2024-2026 Sanctum is default for first-party SPA and API tokens, Passport only for
third-party OAuth apps
- C) Sanctum is OAuth2
- D) Passport is deprecated fully
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Sanctum issues plain personal access tokens stored hashed in DB, authenticated via Bearer, ideal for mobile
apps. Passport implements full OAuth2 authorization code flow with clients and scopes, needed when you are an OAuth
provider like GitHub.
Q29. Design a scalable event-driven architecture in Laravel: Events,
Listeners, ShouldBroadcast, and new Event Broadcasting vs Queued
Closures.
- A) Put all logic in controller
- B) Dispatch OrderPlaced event → multiple listeners ShouldQueue (SendEmail, DeductInventory), broadcast via
ShouldBroadcast over Reverb/Pusher, use Event::fake() in tests, transactional events via DB::transaction + afterCommit()
- C) Events are for frontend only
- D) Listeners cannot be queued
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Events decouple side-effects via observer pattern; queued listeners run async. Laravel Reverb (2024) is
first-party WebSocket server replacing Pusher for broadcasting. Transactional outbox pattern via ShouldDispatchAfterCommit
ensures events fire only after DB commit.
Q30. How do you optimize Laravel for production in 2024-2026 stack:
Octane, FrankenPHP, OPcache, config/route/view/event caching?
- A) No optimization needed
- B) Run composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader, php artisan config:cache route:cache view:cache event:cache,
enable OPcache JIT opcache.jit_buffer_size=100M, use Octane with FrankenPHP/RoadRunner for worker mode, use php
artisan optimize, CDN + Redis cache, and SCOUT, telescope disabled in prod
- C) Only debug mode
- D) Use dd() in production
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Caching config/routes/views converts PHP files to serialized cache preventing disk scans. Octane with
FrankenPHP (Laravel's new default in 2024+ installer) keeps app booted in memory cutting 50-80% latency. OPcache JIT
compiles hot paths to machine code.

# Exam 21: Vue.js & Angular
**Level:** Comprehensive (Beginner to Advanced)
**Total Questions:** 30
**Passing Score:** 70%
**Time Limit:** 60 minutes
**Stack Coverage:** Vue 3.4+ (Composition API, <script setup>, Pinia), Angular 17-19 (Standalone, Signals, Control Flow),
TypeScript, Vite/Angular CLI, SSR
Part 1: Beginner (10 Questions)
Q1: What is the primary difference between Vue's Options API and
Composition API?
A) Options API is only for Vue 2, Composition API is only for Vue 3
B) Options API organizes code by option type (data, methods, computed), while Composition API organizes code by logical
concern using setup() and composables
C) Composition API does not support reactivity
D) Options API is faster than Composition API
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Options API groups logic by option type which can scatter related logic. Composition API uses ref, reactive,
and composables inside setup() to co-locate related logic, improving reusability and TypeScript inference.
Q2: In Vue 3, which is the correct way to create a reactive primitive count?
A) let count = reactive(0)
B) const count = ref(0) and access via count.value in script
C) const count = data(0)
D) const count = reactive({ value: 0 }) is the only way
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** ref() is designed for primitives and returns an object with a .value property that is reactive. reactive() only
works with objects and cannot hold primitives directly.
Q3: In Angular 17+, what does it mean for a Component to be standalone:
true?
A) It cannot import other components
B) It does not need to be declared in an NgModule and can directly import its dependencies via the imports array
C) It automatically becomes a lazy-loaded route
D) It is only usable in tests
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Standalone components, now the default in Angular 17+, are self-contained and declare their own
dependencies. This eliminates the need for NgModules for most applications and simplifies the mental model.
Q4: What does v-model do in Vue 3?
A) One-way data binding from parent to child only
B) Creates a two-way binding, equivalent to :modelValue prop + @update:modelValue event
C) Only works on native input elements

--- PAGE 145 ---
D) Is deprecated in Vue 3
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** v-model is syntactic sugar for two-way binding. In Vue 3, it defaults to modelValue prop and
update:modelValue event, and can be customized with arguments like v-model:title.
Q5: Which Angular decorator is used to pass data from parent to child
component?
A) @Output()
B) @Input()
C) @Inject()
D) @ViewChild()
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** @Input() marks a property as input-bound, allowing a parent template to bind data like [user]="currentUser".
@Output() is the opposite direction for child-to-parent events via EventEmitter.
Q6: In Vue 3 <script setup> what does defineProps<{ msg: string }>() do?
A) Defines a reactive state variable
B) It is a compile-time macro that declares props with full TypeScript type inference without needing to import it
C) Creates a Pinia store
D) Defines emits
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** defineProps and defineEmits are compiler macros in <script setup>, not real imports. They provide typed
props with zero runtime overhead and are automatically available.
Q7: What is Angular's new built-in control flow introduced in Angular 17?
A) *ngIf and *ngFor with better performance
B) @if, @for, @switch block syntax with @empty and tracking
C) v-if and v-for
D) NgIf directive renamed to If
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Angular 17 introduced block syntax like @if (condition) {} @else {} and @for (item of items; track item.id).
This syntax is more performant, does not need CommonModule, and enables better type narrowing.
Q8: What is Pinia in the Vue ecosystem?
A) The official Vue Router replacement
B) The official state management library for Vue 3, replacing Vuex, with Composition API style stores
C) A testing library for Vue
D) A CSS framework
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Pinia is now the officially recommended store for Vue, with a simpler API than Vuex using defineStore(). It
has native TypeScript support, devtools, and supports both Options and Setup syntax.
Q9: How do you create a reactive signal in Angular 17+?
A) const count = ref(0)
B) count = signal(0) and read via count() and update via count.set() or count.update()
C) count = new BehaviorSubject(0)

--- PAGE 146 ---
D) count = reactive(0)
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Angular Signals introduced via signal() provide fine-grained reactivity. You read the value by calling the
signal as a function count() and mutate it explicitly, enabling zoneless change detection in future.
Q10: In Vue, what is the purpose of :key in v-for?
A) It is only for styling
B) It gives Vue a unique identifier to track each node for efficient DOM diffing and state preservation
C) It makes the loop run faster by default
D) It is required only for objects, not arrays
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The key attribute helps Vue's virtual DOM algorithm identify nodes when the list order changes. Without
proper keys, component state can be incorrectly preserved leading to bugs.
Part 2: Intermediate (10 Questions)
Q11: In Vue 3, what is the difference between ref and shallowRef?
A) No difference
B) ref makes its value deeply reactive, while shallowRef only tracks .value replacement, not deep mutations inside the object
C) shallowRef cannot be used in templates
D) ref is only for primitives
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** ref() wraps objects with reactive() making nested properties reactive. shallowRef() avoids deep conversion
for performance when handling large objects or external libraries where you manually trigger updates.
Q12: In Angular, what does inject() function do vs constructor injection?
A) inject() can only be used inside components
B) inject() is the function-based DI API usable in any injection context (constructor, factory, inject() fields, functional
guards/interceptors), enabling more ergonomic composition without constructor boilerplate
C) Constructor injection is deprecated
D) inject() does not support tree-shaking
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** inject() introduced in Angular 14 is preferred for functional APIs like functional route guards and interceptors.
It works in constructors, field initializers, and factory functions, making DI more flexible in Standalone world.
Q13: Consider Vue 3: const props = defineProps<{ items: string[] }>(). Why
should you NOT destructure const { items } = props directly?
A) It is fine, it stays reactive
B) Destructuring loses reactivity in standard props; you should use toRefs(props) or in Vue 3.3+ reactive destructure with
default defineProps maintains reactivity but plain destructure still loses it unless enabled
C) It causes memory leak
D) Props cannot be arrays
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Props are a reactive object; plain destructuring extracts plain values and breaks the proxy link. Vue 3.5
introduced reactive props destructure where const { items } = defineProps<...>() does retain reactivity, but general rule still
applies to avoid losing reactivity.

--- PAGE 147 ---
Q14: In Angular, how do you handle derived state with Signals?
A) Use computed(() => count() * 2) to create a memoised derived signal
B) Use ngOnChanges
C) Use map() operator
D) Use derived()
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** computed() creates a read-only signal that automatically recomputes when its dependencies change. It is
lazy, memoised, and glitch-free, replacing many manual observable pipelines.
Q15: What is the purpose of defineModel() introduced in Vue 3.4?
A) To define database models
B) A macro to simplify v-model two-way binding definition in child components, replacing manual defineProps + defineEmits
for modelValue
C) To define Pinia models
D) To define Angular models
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** const model = defineModel() returns a ref synced to the parent's v-model. It can take modifiers and type,
e.g., defineModel<string>('title'), drastically reducing boilerplate for custom v-model components.
Q16: In Angular, what is defer block (Angular 17+)?
A) It defers TypeScript compilation
B) @defer { <big-component /> } lazily loads and renders the content when a trigger (idle, viewport, interaction, timer) is met,
improving initial bundle and INP
C) It is same as *ngIf
D) It delays HTTP requests
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Deferrable views enable declarative lazy loading at template level without routing. Triggers like @defer (on
viewport) load heavy components only when needed, automatically code-splitting them.
Q17: In Vue 3 Composition API, when should you use watchEffect vs
watch?
A) They are identical
B) watchEffect auto-tracks dependencies and runs immediately, while watch is explicit about source and can be lazy; watch is
better for specific source and old/new values
C) watch cannot watch reactive objects
D) watchEffect is only for props
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** watchEffect() immediately runs and tracks any reactive used inside. watch(source, cb) watches explicitly
defined sources, offers oldValue, and is lazy by default unless immediate:true, giving more control.
Q18: How does Angular's new input() signal-based API (Angular 17.1+)
differ from @Input()?
A) It is just alias
B) userName = input<string>() creates a signal input, read as userName() with options input.required<>() and transforms,
integrating with signal reactivity ecosystem
C) It cannot be used with TypeScript

--- PAGE 148 ---
D) It removes change detection
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Signal inputs are the signal evolution of @Input(), providing InputSignal that participates in computed/effect.
They support required, alias, and transform, offering stronger typing than decorator version.
Q19: In Vue, what does <Suspense> do?
A) Suspends rendering forever
B) Handles async dependencies in component tree, showing #fallback slot while async setup() or async components are
resolving
C) Only works with Angular
D) Is deprecated
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** <Suspense> is built-in for orchestrating async components, commonly used with async <script setup> or
defineAsyncComponent(). It enables top-level await inside setup and better loading states.
Q20: What is Angular DestroyRef and why use it over ngOnDestroy in
composables?
A) It destroys components manually
B) It provides destroyRef.onDestroy(() => cleanup) in any injection context, allowing cleanup logic in functions/composables
without needing a component class implementing OnDestroy
C) It is only for RxJS
D) It replaces all lifecycle hooks
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** DestroyRef enables composition-style cleanup in functional contexts like custom composables or inject()
services. It decouples cleanup from component class, making reusable logic that auto-cleans on destroy possible.
Part 3: Advanced (10 Questions)
Q21: Vue 3.4+ reactivity optimization - How do ref and its internal
implementation improve performance?
A) Vue 3.4 rewrote reactivity to use doubly-linked dep tracking and optimized ref unwrapping, making ref() getters much
cheaper and avoiding unnecessary effect triggers
B) It removed Proxy
C) It uses polling
D) ref is now sync
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Vue 3.4's reactivity refactor improved tracking efficiency with faster dep linked lists and more cases where
access doesn't trigger tracking. This reduces overhead for large reactive graphs and improves computed performance
significantly.
Q22: In Angular, what is an effect() and what is a crucial anti-pattern?
A) Effect runs after each render like watchEffect, but you should NOT write to signals inside effects to create infinite loops;
use computed for derived state and effect only for side-effects like logging/manual DOM
B) Effect is same as computed and can set signals
C) Effect runs synchronously before rendering
D) Effect replaces all subscriptions
**Answer: A**

--- PAGE 149 ---
**Explanation:** effect(() => { console.log(count()) }) runs when its signal dependencies change. Writing to signals inside
effects is discouraged and Angular warns; allowable writes require allowSignalWrites:true, as it breaks predictable data flow.
Q23: Explain Vue's defineAsyncComponent advanced options for
production hardening.
A) It only takes a loader function
B) You can provide loader, loadingComponent, errorComponent, delay, timeout, suspensible: false to control loading UX and
error fallback for code-split components
C) It cannot be used with Vite
D) Async components never need error handling
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** For production, async components need robust loading and error states. timeout aborts slow loads, delay
avoids flashing, and errorComponent handles import failures like network chunk load errors.
Q24: In Angular SSR and Hydration (v17-19), what does
withEnabledBlockingInitialNavigation and non-destructive hydration
mean?
A) SSR is not supported
B) Angular's hydration reuses server-rendered DOM instead of re-rendering, and in v19 hydration supports withI18nSupport
and better de-hydration for @defer; mismatch causes NG0500 warning
C) Hydration always destroys DOM
D) SSR only works with NgModules
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Angular's hydration matches server DOM to client component tree, preserving nodes for faster FCP and no
flicker. Newer versions handle @defer, i18n blocks, and event replay, but DOM manipulation before hydration breaks it.
Q25: How does Vite handle Vue SFC vs Angular standalone compilation?
A) Both use webpack
B) Vite uses @vitejs/plugin-vue with compiler-sfc to compile <template> into render functions via HMR, while Angular uses
@analogjs/vite-plugin-angular / official esbuild-based application builder with AOT and standalone incremental compilation
C) Vite cannot compile Vue
D) Angular cannot use Vite
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Vue's SFC pipeline compiles template+script+style to JS with hot replacement. Angular's new application
builder (esbuild+bundler) AOT-compiles templates to JS instructions, supports Vite dev server via Analog or Angular's own
Vite integration since Angular 17.
Q26: In Pinia, what is the difference between store.$patch() vs direct
mutation vs store.$state =?
A) All same
B) $patch({ count: 1 }) or $patch(state => state.count++) batches multiple mutations into one devtools entry and subscriber
notification, direct mutation is fine in setup stores, while replacing $state replaces whole state but must preserve reactivity
C) $patch is deprecated
D) Direct mutation is forbidden
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** $patch enables atomic batch updates reducing rerenders and grouping undo history. In Setup stores
(defineStore('id', () => { const count = ref(0); return {count} })) you can mutate directly count.value++, which is idiomatic.

--- PAGE 150 ---
Q27: Angular performance: When would you use
ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush with Signals vs Default?
A) OnPush is deprecated with Signals
B) With Signals, Angular can mark components dirty only when signal inputs or template-read signals change, making
OnPush nearly automatic and enabling future zoneless; default still checks every async event, while OnPush + signals
drastically reduces change detection cycles
C) OnPush makes it slower
D) OnPush only works with RxJS
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** OnPush tells Angular to skip components unless Inputs changed, events fired, or markForCheck. Signals
enhance this by precisely tracking template dependencies, so only components reading a changed signal are marked, paving
way for provideExperimentalZonelessChangeDetection.
Q28: Vue advanced: What are Vapor mode and defineOptions()?
A) Vapor is CSS framework
B) Vapor (experimental, Vue 3.4+) is a no-virtual-DOM compilation strategy where templates compile to direct DOM
operations for performance; defineOptions({ name, inheritAttrs:false }) sets component options inside <script setup>
C) Vapor removes reactivity
D) defineOptions is for Angular
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Vapor mode aims to outperform vDOM by generating imperative DOM code similar to Solid.js. defineOptions
solves the need to add a separate <script> block just to set name or inheritAttrs in <script setup>.
Q29: Angular advanced DI: Explain EnvironmentInjector,
runInInjectionContext, and why needed for composable-like functions?
A) They are not needed
B) runInInjectionContext(envInjector, () => useMyComposable()) allows using inject(), signal(), computed() outside
constructor context, essential for utility functions that use DI but are called outside injection context (e.g., in RxJS operators)
C) It replaces constructor
D) EnvironmentInjector is same as Root
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Angular's inject() only works in injection context. When you extract logic to standalone functions (similar to
Vue composables), you need an injector context passed in. runInInjectionContext temporarily sets context so the composable
can call inject(HttpClient) or inject(DestroyRef).
Q30: SSR data fetching: Compare Vue's useAsyncData (Nuxt 3) vs
Angular's resolve + TransferState.
A) They are identical
B) Nuxt useAsyncData(key, fetcher) fetches on server, serializes payload into hydration bundle to avoid re-fetch on client;
Angular uses route resolvers or inject(TransferState) / makeStateKey and HttpClient with withCache() / withFetch to transfer
HTTP cache from server to client
C) SSR does not need data transfer
D) Angular cannot transfer state
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Both solve hydration double-fetch problem. Nuxt automatically handles payload via __NUXT_DATA__.
Angular uses TransferState or new HttpTransferCacheOptions (withHttpTransferCache) to capture server HTTP requests and
replay on client, preventing extra API calls.

Course File: 22_React.md
# 22_React - React & Frontend Exam
**Total Questions: 30 | Beginner: 10 | Intermediate: 10 | Advanced: 10**
Part 1: Beginner (Questions 1-10)
1. What does JSX stand for and what does it compile to?
A) Java Syntax Extension, compiles to Java bytecode
B) JavaScript XML, compiles to React.createElement() calls
C) JavaScript Extension, compiles to HTML strings
D) JSON XML, compiles to JSON objects
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** JSX is JavaScript XML, a syntax extension that looks like HTML in JS. It is transpiled by Babel/SWC into
React.createElement(type, props, ...children) calls, or _jsx() runtime calls in the new JSX transform.
2. Which hook is used to manage local state in a functional component?
A) useEffect
B) useState
C) useMemo
D) useRef
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** useState declares a state variable and setter that triggers re-renders when updated. It is the primary way to
manage local component state in function components since React 16.8.
3. What is the purpose of the key prop when rendering lists in React?
A) To style list items uniquely
B) To help React identify which items have changed, added, or removed for reconciliation
C) To make the list searchable
D) To encrypt list data
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Keys give elements a stable identity across renders, allowing React's diffing algorithm to reuse DOM nodes
efficiently. Using index as key can cause bugs when list order changes because identity is lost.
4. In React, data flows:
A) Bidirectionally between all components
B) From child to parent only
C) Unidirectionally from parent to child via props
D) Randomly based on useEffect
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** React enforces one-way data flow where parent components pass data down to children via props.
Child-to-parent communication is done by passing callback functions as props, not by direct two-way binding.
5. What will happen if you directly mutate state like state.count = 5 instead
of using setState/setCount?
A) Component will re-render immediately

--- PAGE 153 ---
B) React will throw a compile-time error
C) State will change but React will not detect it, so no re-render will occur
D) It will work identically to setCount
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** React compares state references to decide to re-render; direct mutation keeps the same reference so React
bails out. Always use the setter which creates a new reference and schedules an update.
6. Which hook is used for side effects like data fetching, subscriptions, or
DOM manipulation after render?
A) useState
B) useReducer
C) useEffect
D) useId
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** useEffect runs after the render commits to the DOM and is meant for side effects. It optionally returns a
cleanup function and is controlled by its dependency array.
7. What is a controlled component in React?
A) A component controlled by the browser only
B) A form input whose value is controlled by React state via value and onChange
C) A component that cannot be reused
D) A component wrapped in React.memo
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In a controlled component, React state is the single source of truth for the input value, updated on every
onChange. This contrasts with uncontrolled components that use refs to read values imperatively.
8. What does useRef return and does updating it cause a re-render?
A) Returns state and causes re-render
B) Returns a mutable { current: ... } object that persists across renders and does NOT cause re-render when mutated
C) Returns a promise and causes re-render
D) Returns a DOM node only
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** useRef returns a mutable ref object whose .current can hold any value and survives re-renders. Changing
.current does not trigger a re-render, making it ideal for DOM refs and storing timers/intervals.
9. What is the correct way to conditionally render in JSX?
A) if (show) { <Component/> } directly inside JSX return
B) show && <Component /> or ternary show ? <Component /> : null
C) show then <Component />
D) Using HTML if attribute
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** JSX is JavaScript expressions, so you must use expression forms like && short-circuit or ternary inside {}.
Statement-level if cannot be placed directly inside JSX markup, but can be used before the return.
10. Why does React require onClick instead of onclick?
A) React does not support click events

--- PAGE 154 ---
B) React uses SyntheticEvent system with camelCase naming to normalize cross-browser behavior
C) onclick is deprecated in HTML
D) To save bytes
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** React wraps native events in SyntheticEvent for cross-browser consistency and uses camelCase JSX
convention. Since React 17, events are attached at the root rather than document, but the camelCase API remains.
Part 2: Intermediate (Questions 11-20)
11. What is the difference between useEffect, useLayoutEffect, and
useInsertionEffect?
A) They are identical
B) useEffect runs asynchronously after paint, useLayoutEffect runs synchronously after DOM mutations but before paint,
useInsertionEffect runs synchronously before any DOM mutations for CSS-in-JS insertion
C) useEffect runs before render
D) Only useLayoutEffect exists in React 18+
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** useInsertionEffect is for CSS-in-JS libraries to inject styles before layout calculations. useLayoutEffect blocks
paint and is for DOM measurements, while useEffect is deferred and non-blocking for most side effects.
12. In React 18, what does automatic batching mean?
A) Multiple setState calls in promises, setTimeout, and native event handlers are now batched into a single re-render
B) React batches only inside React event handlers like before
C) Batching was removed in React 18
D) Batching only works with class components
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Before React 18, batching only happened in React event handlers; now createRoot automatically batches all
updates regardless of origin. This reduces unnecessary renders and improves performance out of the box.
13. What is React.memo and when should you use it?
A) A hook to memoize state
B) A Higher-Order Component that memoizes the rendered output and skips re-rendering if props are shallowly equal
C) It memoizes useEffect automatically
D) It is required for all components
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** React.memo performs shallow prop comparison to avoid re-rendering a component when props haven't
changed. Overusing it can hurt performance due to comparison cost, so use it for expensive components with stable props.
14. Explain the rules of hooks.
A) Hooks can be called anywhere
B) Only call hooks at the top level of React functions and only from React function components or custom hooks; never in
loops, conditions, or nested functions
C) Hooks must be called inside class components
D) Hooks order doesn't matter
**Answer: B**

--- PAGE 155 ---
**Explanation:** React relies on call order to associate hook state with component instances, so conditional hook calls would
break order. The eslint-plugin-react-hooks enforces these rules statically.
15. What is the purpose of useCallback and useMemo? How do they differ?
A) They are the same
B) useMemo memoizes a computed value, useCallback memoizes a function reference; both optimize child re-renders when
passing props to memoized components
C) useCallback fetches data
D) useMemo is for side effects
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** useMemo(() => compute(a,b), [a,b]) caches the result, while useCallback(fn, deps) is syntactic sugar for
useMemo(() => fn, deps). They help prevent referential identity changes that would break React.memo optimizations.
16. What are React Server Components (RSC) introduced in React 18 /
Next.js 13+?
A) Components that only run inside useEffect
B) Components that render on the server, send zero JS to client by default, can be async, and cannot use
useState/useEffect; client components are marked with 'use client'
C) Components that replace all client components
D) Old name for SSR
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** RSCs run exclusively on the server, allowing direct async data fetching and access to backend resources
without client bundle cost. They interoperate with Client Components via props/children, with file-level 'use client' / 'use server'
directives defining boundaries.
17. What is the difference between useDeferredValue and useTransition in
React 18/19?
A) No difference
B) useTransition wraps a state update as low-priority and returns [isPending, startTransition] to control when;
useDeferredValue defers a value itself, creating a lagging version with low priority
C) useDeferredValue is for server only
D) Both are deprecated
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** useTransition gives imperative control over marking updates as transitions (great for input -> filtering).
useDeferredValue(value) gives a declarative deferred version of a value, useful when you don't control the update call site,
like prop passed from parent.
18. In Next.js 14/15 App Router, what does loading.js, error.js, and
not-found.js do?
A) Nothing, they are ignored
B) They are special file conventions: loading.js renders as Suspense fallback for that segment, error.js is an error boundary
('use client' required), not-found.js renders when notFound() is called or route has no match
C) They only work in pages/ router
D) They must be server components only
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** App Router uses file-system conventions for UI states within each route segment. loading.js auto-wraps
page.js in Suspense, error.js must be a Client Component to catch errors, enabling nested, streaming UX without manual

--- PAGE 156 ---
boilerplate.
19. What potential issue does this useEffect have? useEffect(() => {
fetch(url).then(setData) }, [url]) with rapid url changes
A) No issue
B) Race condition: older fetch may resolve after newer one and overwrite with stale data; need AbortController or ignoring
stale responses, cleanup function
C) It causes infinite loop always
D) setData is not allowed
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Rapid URL prop changes trigger overlapping fetches where completion order is not guaranteed. Fix by using
an AbortController in cleanup (return () => controller.abort()) or a let cancelled = false flag to ignore stale results.
20. What is hydration mismatch and how do you avoid it in Next.js / React
18?
A) When CSS fails
B) When server-rendered HTML does not match client initial render due to non-deterministic values like Date.now(),
Math.random(), window access, or locale differences; causes React to warn and re-render entire tree
C) When you forget key prop
D) When using useState
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** SSR requires server and first client render to produce identical markup. Avoid by rendering non-deterministic
values only in useEffect, using suppressHydrationWarning, or dynamically importing client-only libs with ssr: false /
next/dynamic.
Part 3: Advanced (Questions 21-30)
21. In React 19 (2024-2025), what are Actions, useActionState, and
useFormStatus?
A) Redux actions only
B) React 19 natively integrates async actions for form handling: <form action={formAction}> can call server functions ('use
server'), useActionState (formerly useFormState) manages async action result/pending, useFormStatus reads pending state
of parent form
C) They are removed in React 19
D) They replace useEffect
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** React 19 treats form actions as first-class: Server Actions can mutate data and are progressively enhanced.
useActionState returns [state, action, isPending] for UI feedback, and useFormStatus enables design-system components to
disable/submit without prop drilling.
22. How does the React Compiler (React Forget, beta in 2024-2025) change
memoization needs?
A) It removes React
B) It auto-memoizes components and values by analyzing reactivity, making manual useMemo, useCallback, React.memo
largely unnecessary while preserving semantics
C) It requires more useMemo
D) It only compiles CSS

--- PAGE 157 ---
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The React Compiler automatically inserts memoization equivalent to manual useMemo/useCallback at build
time via static analysis. This allows writing code without manual optimization ceremony while achieving same skip-re-render
benefits, though mental model of referential identity still matters.
23. Explain Concurrent Rendering, time-slicing, and how startTransition +
Suspense work together for data fetching.
A) Concurrent means multi-threaded JS
B) Concurrent rendering allows React to interrupt, pause, and resume rendering work; startTransition marks updates as
non-urgent so Suspense can show fallback without blocking typed input, enabling streaming SSR and selective hydration
C) It makes React synchronous only
D) Suspense doesn't work with transitions
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** React's concurrent renderer uses a fiber scheduler that can yield to main thread, keeping UI responsive.
Transitions tell React current UI can stay interactive while new UI with suspended data prepares in background, crucial for
Next.js streaming and use hook patterns.
24. You have a context that updates frequently (e.g., mouse position) and
causes all consumers to re-render. How do you optimize?
A) You cannot optimize context
B) Split contexts (state vs dispatch), use useMemo for context value stability, use useContextSelector pattern / external store
like Zustand/Jotai, or useSyncExternalStore to subscribe granularly; React 19 context is fast but still propagation-based
C) Just use useState everywhere
D) Put everything in one context
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Context uses reference identity; any value change re-renders all consumers even if they use only part.
Splitting into multiple contexts or using selector-based stores prevents large subtree re-renders, which is critical for
high-frequency updates.
25. What is the difference between useEffectEvent (React 19 experimental
-> stable useEffectEvent) and useCallback for event handlers inside
effects?
A) Same thing
B) useEffectEvent (previously useEffectEvent) creates a function with always-latest closure that is NOT a dependency of
effects, solving stale closure vs exhaustive-deps dilemma without re-triggering effect
C) useEffectEvent causes re-render
D) useCallback is not reactive
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Effects often need to call callbacks that shouldn't trigger re-execution; adding them to deps causes loops,
omitting lies. useEffectEvent extracts non-reactive logic with latest props/state but stable identity that doesn't affect deps,
officially solving the pattern formerly solved by refs.
26. Deep dive: Why does setState inside render cause infinite loop, but
useSyncExternalStore and useDeferredValue do not? And what is tearing
in concurrent mode?
A) All cause infinite loops

--- PAGE 158 ---
B) Setting state during render in same component re-triggers render synchronously leading to loop; useSyncExternalStore
subscribes to external store with snapshot consistency guarantees, and useDeferredValue defers via concurrent feature.
Tearing is when different components see different versions of external mutable data due to interruptible render
C) Tearing never happens in React
D) useSyncExternalStore is deprecated
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Render must be pure. useSyncExternalStore uses internal mechanisms to force consistent snapshot and
resubscribe, preventing tearing where UI shows inconsistent state from concurrent partial renders. This was the fix for
problems with raw external stores in React 18 concurrent features.
27. In Next.js 15 (2024-2025) what changed for fetch, caching, and async
params/searchParams? Explain.
A) Nothing changed
B) Next.js 15: fetch is no longer cached by default (previously force-cache), GET Route Handlers not cached by default,
params and searchParams are now Promises (must await), and headers() cookies() etc are async. Opt into cache via
fetch(..., {cache: 'force-cache'}) or revalidate
C) Fetch is removed
D) params are synchronous still
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** To make caching explicit and avoid stale data surprises, Next.js 15 reversed defaults to uncached fetch.
Making params async enables parent layouts to stream and allows React to handle dynamic APIs via use and Suspense
without blocking whole route, aligning with React 19 async primitives.
28. Compare bundle optimization strategies: code splitting with React.lazy
+ Suspense, dynamic import(), Next.js dynamic(), and Server Components.
A) All identical
B) React.lazy code-splits client bundles with Suspense fallback using static import(), Next dynamic() extends it with ssr:false,
loading, and Server Components eliminate JS entirely for server-only parts eliminating need to ship/hydrate that code,
together achieving optimal RSC + client chunk graph
C) Server Components increase bundle size
D) Lazy loading is bad for performance
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Client-side lazy reduces initial JS via separate chunks loaded on demand. RSC goes further by never adding
server-only dependencies (e.g., markdown parser, DB client) to client bundle at all. Best practice in 2024-2026 is RSC for
data/expensive libs, dynamic for heavy client libs like charts.
29. How would you implement a correct global state with useReducer +
Context that survives RSC boundaries and supports performance?
Discuss alternatives like Zustand, Jotai, Redux Toolkit.
A) Use one huge context
B) Create separate StateContext and DispatchContext to avoid re-renders, memoize provider value, use useReducer or
useSyncExternalStore adapter; but for large apps prefer Zustand (external store + selector), Jotai (atomic), or Redux Toolkit
with next-redux-wrapper or RSC-safe provider as client component. Never put mutable global store inside Server Component
C) Context works fine in Server Components
D) Redux cannot be used with Next.js
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Context + useReducer naive combo re-renders all consumers on any state change due to value identity.
Splitting contexts isolates dispatch stability (dispatch never changes). External stores like Zustand use useSyncExternalStore
under the hood, allowing fine-grained selector subscriptions and remaining RSC-compatible as 'use client' providers.

--- PAGE 159 ---
30. Explain strict effects double-mount in React 18 StrictMode, why
useEffect cleanup must be idempotent, and how to test.
A) It's a bug
B) In dev, StrictMode mounts, unmounts (cleanup), then remounts to detect unsafe side effects and non-idempotent setup.
Cleanup must unsubscribe/abort correctly. Test by ensuring fetch abort, subscriptions cleanup, no duplicate listeners; use act
and verify effect doesn't leak after unmount
C) StrictMode is production only
D) Cleanup is optional
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The intentional double-invoke surfaces components relying on once-only mount logic that will break with
future offscreen/keep-alive features. Writing cleanup that reverses setup completely (idempotent) ensures compatibility with
concurrent features where React may discard and re-create trees.

Course File: 23_DevOps.md
Course: DevOps Fundamentals
1. Beginner Level (10 Questions)
**Q1. What is the primary goal of DevOps?**
A. To replace developers with operations engineers
B. To bridge development and operations for faster, more reliable software delivery
C. To automate only infrastructure provisioning
D. To enforce strict separation between Dev and Ops teams
**Q2. In a CI/CD pipeline, what does CI stand for and what does it do?**
A. Continuous Integration - developers frequently merge code into a shared repository with automated builds and tests
B. Continuous Inspection - manually reviewing code once per month
C. Code Isolation - keeping each developer's branch separate forever
D. Central Integration - merging all code only on release day
**Q3. What is Infrastructure as Code (IaC)?**
A. Writing application code inside infrastructure servers
B. Managing and provisioning infrastructure through machine-readable definition files instead of manual configuration
C. Using physical hardware to run code
D. A tool that converts infrastructure into application code
**Q4. Which tool is commonly used for version control in DevOps workflows?**
A. Docker
B. Jenkins
C. Git
D. Nagios
**Q5. What is the purpose of a Dockerfile?**
A. To configure a Linux firewall
B. To define instructions for building a Docker container image
C. To store database migrations
D. To monitor container logs
**Q6. What does CD mean in a DevOps context? (Continuous Delivery / Continuous Deployment)**
A. Only manual deployment to production once a year
B. Automatically ensuring code is always in a deployable state and can be released to production automatically or with one
click
C. Copying code from one developer laptop to another
D. Continuous Debugging of production servers
**Q7. You want to run the same application consistently across dev, test, and prod environments. Which concept helps you
achieve this?**
A. Configuration Drift
B. Containerization with images like Docker
C. Manual server setup with documentation
D. Snowflake servers
**Q8. What is the role of monitoring and logging in DevOps?**
A. They are only needed after the product is retired
B. They provide feedback on application health, performance, and failures to enable fast detection and recovery

--- PAGE 161 ---
C. They replace the need for testing
D. They are used only by the security team
**Q9. In Git workflow, what is a pull request (or merge request)?**
A. A request to delete the main branch
B. A mechanism to propose, review, and discuss code changes before merging into a target branch
C. A backup of the repository
D. An automated deployment to production
**Q10. What is the difference between a virtual machine (VM) and a container?**
A. There is no difference
B. VMs virtualize hardware with a full guest OS per instance, containers share the host OS kernel and are lightweight and
faster to start
C. Containers are heavier than VMs and include a full OS
D. VMs cannot run in the cloud, containers can
Beginner Answer Key & Explanations
**Q1 Answer: B - To bridge development and operations for faster, more reliable software delivery**
DevOps combines cultural practices, automation, and shared responsibility to shorten development cycles. It breaks down
silos between Dev and Ops rather than reinforcing them.
**Q2 Answer: A - Continuous Integration - developers frequently merge code into a shared repository with automated builds
and tests**
CI runs automated builds and tests on every push to catch integration issues early. This prevents the "merge hell" that occurs
with long-lived branches.
**Q3 Answer: B - Managing and provisioning infrastructure through machine-readable definition files instead of manual
configuration**
IaC tools like Terraform, Pulumi, and CloudFormation make infrastructure versioned, repeatable, and reviewable. It eliminates
manual click-ops and reduces configuration drift.
**Q4 Answer: C - Git**
Git is the de facto distributed version control system used in almost all DevOps pipelines. Jenkins is CI, Docker is containers,
and Nagios is monitoring.
**Q5 Answer: B - To define instructions for building a Docker container image**
A Dockerfile contains FROM, RUN, COPY, CMD etc. to declaratively build an immutable image. This image ensures
consistent runtime across environments.
**Q6 Answer: B - Automatically ensuring code is always in a deployable state and can be released to production
automatically or with one click**
Continuous Delivery means artifact is production-ready at any time; Continuous Deployment automates the release itself.
Both rely on automated tests and deployment pipelines.
**Q7 Answer: B - Containerization with images like Docker**
Container images package code and dependencies together, ensuring parity from laptop to production. This solves "it works
on my machine" problems caused by snowflake environments.
**Q8 Answer: B - They provide feedback on application health, performance, and failures to enable fast detection and
recovery**
The three pillars of observability (metrics, logs, traces) enable MTTR reduction and SRE practices. Tools like Prometheus,
Grafana, Loki, and OpenTelemetry are standard in 2024-2026.
**Q9 Answer: B - A mechanism to propose, review, and discuss code changes before merging into a target branch**
Pull requests enable code review, automated checks, and knowledge sharing. They are a core collaboration practice in
trunk-based and GitFlow workflows.
**Q10 Answer: B - VMs virtualize hardware with a full guest OS per instance, containers share the host OS kernel and are
lightweight and faster to start**

--- PAGE 162 ---
Containers start in seconds and use fewer resources due to shared kernel. VMs provide stronger hardware-level isolation but
with higher overhead.

the console. On the next terraform apply, what will happen and what's the best practice?**
A. Terraform will ignore the change; manual changes are preferred
B. Terraform will detect drift and propose to revert the instance type to the code-defined state; best practice is to prohibit
manual changes and use terraform plan + code review
C. Terraform will automatically delete the instance
D. Terraform state will corrupt and require reinstall
**Q12. You have a Jenkins/GitHub Actions pipeline that builds Docker images. Builds are slow due to reinstalling
dependencies every time. Which caching strategy should you implement as of 2024-2026?**
A. Delete Docker layer cache each build for cleanliness
B. Use Docker BuildKit cache mounts (--mount=type=cache) for package managers, registry layer caching with buildx
--cache-from/to, and GitHub Actions cache for actions/cache
C. Copy entire node_modules into the repo
D. Use docker system prune -a before each build
**Q13. You need to manage secrets for a Kubernetes workload. Which approach aligns with DevOps security best practices
in 2025?**
A. Hardcode secrets in Dockerfile ENV and push to GitHub
B. Use Kubernetes Secrets encrypted at rest with KMS, or better, external secrets management via External Secrets
Operator with HashiCorp Vault or AWS Secrets Manager / SOPS sealed secrets, with no secrets in Git
C. Store secrets in a public ConfigMap
D. Pass secrets via HTTP query parameters
**Q14. Your microservices deployment uses blue-green deployment. What is its key advantage over rolling deployment for
critical checkout service?**
A. It uses less resources
B. It keeps two identical production environments, allowing instant rollback by switching router/service, and zero-downtime
with full version testing before cutover
C. It is the same as canary deployment
D. It deletes old version before new version starts
**Q15. You are configuring Prometheus monitoring for Kubernetes. Pods are highly ephemeral and IP addresses change
frequently. How should Prometheus discover targets?**
A. Hardcode pod IPs in prometheus.yml
B. Use Kubernetes service discovery (kubernetes_sd_configs) with annotations, and deploy via Operator with ServiceMonitor
CRDs for auto-discovery
C. Manually edit targets every hour
D. Use static file with DNS A records
**Q16. A Dockerfile currently uses FROM ubuntu:latest and RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y nodejs npm and copies
whole repo with COPY . .. Builds are insecure, large, and non-reproducible. What is the 2024-2026 best-practice fix?**
A. Keep as-is, latest is always safe
B. Pin base image digest (FROM ubuntu:22.04@sha256:...), use multi-stage build with distroless or alpine node:20-alpine
final image, use .dockerignore, and run as non-root USER
C. Use FROM scratch and add Ubuntu manually
D. Install everything as root and use latest tag for image
**Q17. Your Terraform monolith has a single state file for 300 resources across dev, staging, prod. Applies take 20 minutes,
and blast radius is high. What should you do?**
A. Keep single state file for simplicity

--- PAGE 163 ---
B. Split into workspaces or directory-per-environment with remote backend (S3 + DynamoDB lock / Terraform Cloud), use
modules, and implement state isolation, and consider Terragrunt or tf stacks
C. Use local state on developer laptop
D. Delete state file to speed up applies
**Q18. In GitHub Actions, you need to avoid long-lived AWS credentials for deploying to EKS. What is the recommended
2024-2026 method?**
A. Store AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID and SECRET in repository code
B. Use OIDC federation: configure GitHub as OIDC provider in AWS IAM, assume role via
aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials with role-to-assume, no static keys
C. Share one IAM user key across all teams
D. Disable all authentication
**Q19. Your services communicate via REST, but deployments cause breaking changes for consumers. Which DevOps
practice plus API strategy mitigates this?**
A. Ignore versioning and force consumers to update
B. Implement contract testing (Pact), API versioning (/v1, /v2), backward compatibility checks in CI, and canary + feature flags
(LaunchDarkly / OpenFeature) to decouple deploy from release
C. Use FTP to share JSON files
D. Deploy all services at exactly midnight without tests
**Q20. You are asked to implement GitOps for Kubernetes. Which tool and workflow matches GitOps principles?**
A. Run kubectl apply manually from laptop on production cluster
B. Use ArgoCD or Flux that continuously reconciles cluster state from Git repository, with Git as single source of truth,
automated drift detection and self-heal, PR-based approvals
C. Use Jenkins to SSH into nodes and run docker run
D. Store YAML files on USB drive
Intermediate Answer Key & Explanations
**Q11 Answer: B - Terraform will detect drift and propose to revert the instance type to the code-defined state**
Terraform compares desired state (code), state file, and actual infrastructure to detect drift. Immutability and banning
click-ops ensures IaC remains the source of truth.
**Q12 Answer: B - Use Docker BuildKit cache mounts and registry layer caching**
BuildKit cache mounts persist package manager caches without bloating image layers, and registry caching reuses layers
across CI runners. This cuts build time by 60-80% compared to fresh installs.
**Q13 Answer: B - Use Kubernetes Secrets encrypted at rest with KMS, or better, external secrets management**
Storing secrets in Git is a critical security failure. ESO or Sealed Secrets with Vault/AWS Secrets Manager ensures
encryption, rotation, and auditability via RBAC.
**Q14 Answer: B - It keeps two identical production environments, allowing instant rollback by switching router/service**
Blue-green minimizes risk for critical services by keeping old (blue) live until new (green) passes smoke tests. Unlike rolling,
rollback is O(1) router flip, not slow progressive re-deploy.
**Q15 Answer: B - Use Kubernetes service discovery with annotations and ServiceMonitor CRDs**
Prometheus kubernetes_sd_configs watches API server for pod/service endpoints, handling churn automatically. Operator's
ServiceMonitor abstracts scrape config without editing prometheus.yml.
**Q16 Answer: B - Pin base image digest, use multi-stage build with distroless or alpine final image**
Pinning digest guarantees reproducible builds and prevents supply-chain attacks via tag mutation. Multi-stage + distroless
reduces attack surface and size by 90% and non-root enforces least privilege.
**Q17 Answer: B - Split into workspaces or directory-per-environment with remote backend**
Single large state file increases lock contention, blast radius, and plan time. Environment isolation via remote backend and
modules follows DRY with Terragrunt patterns used since 2024.
**Q18 Answer: B - Use OIDC federation: configure GitHub as OIDC provider in AWS IAM**

--- PAGE 164 ---
OIDC eliminates static long-lived secrets, providing short-lived tokens per workflow run bound to repo/branch claims. This
aligns with 2024 AWS best practice for CI/CD supply chain security.
**Q19 Answer: B - Implement contract testing, API versioning, backward compatibility checks, and canary + feature flags**
Contract testing catches breaking provider changes in CI before deployment. Versioning + feature flags allow safe
progressive delivery without forcing immediate consumer upgrades.
**Q20 Answer: B - Use ArgoCD or Flux that continuously reconciles cluster state from Git repository**
GitOps requires declarative Git as source of truth and automated agent reconciliation. Tools like ArgoCD provide drift
detection, automated sync, and audit trail from PRs.
3. Advanced Level (10 Questions)
**Q21. You run 50 microservices on Kubernetes. Deployments are frequent, and you see increased 500s during deployments
due to in-flight requests being killed. Your liveness/readiness probes are identical. What production-grade fix is needed in
2025?**
A. Delete liveness probe altogether
B. Configure proper lifecycle: distinct readiness probe that fails during shutdown, preStop hook with sleep 15, set
terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 60, handle SIGTERM gracefully in app, and enable PodDisruptionBudgets and
readinessGates with service mesh / AWS ALB target draining
C. Use kill -9 in preStop
D. Set terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 0 for faster deploys
**Q22. Your organization practices DevSecOps. You need to secure container supply chain: build from untrusted base
images, prevent critical CVEs, and verify image signature at admission to EKS cluster with policy as code. What modern 2025
stack?**
A. No scanning needed, trust Docker Hub latest
B. Use Trivy/Grype in CI for scanning, build with Docker Buildx with SBOM and attestations (--attest type=sbom,provenance),
sign with Cosign / Sigstore keyless from OIDC, store in ECR with immutable tags, enforce with Kyverno/OPA Gatekeeper
policy requiring valid Sigstore signature and no critical CVEs
C. Scan manually once per year
D. Push images with latest tag only
**Q23. You manage Terraform Cloud with teams across time zones merging to main. You need to prevent concurrent applies
corrupting state, enforce policy (no public S3 buckets), and reduce plan time for 1000+ resources. Architecture?**
A. Use local state files shared via Slack
B. Use Terraform Cloud remote execution with speculative plans on PR, state locking via backend, Sentinel/OPA policies for
compliance (e.g., deny acl=public-read), and break into stacks with terraform stack or terragrunt with dependency and use
-target only temporarily, plus parallel plans and move to HCP Terraform with ephemeral workspaces
C. Disable state locking for speed
D. Allow everyone to apply from laptop
**Q24. You are implementing SRE SLOs for a payment API: 99.9% availability over 28-day rolling window, 300ms P95
latency. Error budget is 43 minutes/month. During deployment, burn rate alert fires 10x. How should you architect alerts and
actions per 2024-2026 SRE best practice?**
A. Alert on all errors immediately to page on-call
B. Implement multi-window multi-burn-rate alerts (Google SRE workbook): fast burn (1h and 5m windows) pages immediately
for 10x burn, slow burn (6h and 1h) tickets for 1-2x, wire to error budget policy halting feature releases when budget <10%
and requiring freeze, using OpenSLO / Sloth for SLO as Code
C. Disable all alerting
D. Use average latency only
**Q25. Your CI/CD generates 10k builds/day. You store Docker images in ECR, Helm charts, and Terraform modules.
Developer complains about artifact sprawl, reproducibility failures, and lack of traceability from commit to deployed image.
What platform strategy in 2025-2026?**
A. Keep artifacts in local laptop directories

--- PAGE 165 ---
B. Adopt artifact management with OCI Registry as universal artifact store (ECR/Harbor for images + Helm OCI), use
immutable semantic version tags + commit SHA, generate SBOM + provenance with SLSA Level 3, centralize with Backstage
IDP or Artifactory, garbage collect untagged, and link via Git commit -> image digest -> deployment via ArgoCD tracking
C. Use latest tag for every image
D. Delete all old images manually
**Q26. You have multi-region active-active deployment on Kubernetes with Global Accelerator. You need to support stateful
workloads (PostgreSQL) with zero data loss failover and consistent deploys. How do you handle data layer and deployment
orchestration?**
A. Deploy PostgreSQL as bare pods with hostPath volumes and async rsync
B. Do not replicate database
C. Use cloud-managed multi-region DB (Aurora Global, Cloud Spanner, CockroachDB) or Patroni with synchronous
replication + automated backup, separate data plane from compute deploys, use GitOps ApplicationSets for multi-cluster
progressive delivery with Argo Rollouts and analysis templates checking DB lag
D. Use SQLite shared via NFS
**Q27. You observe configuration drift across 200 EC2 instances managed by Ansible. Some nodes were manually patched.

A. Run Ansible in check mode only once a year

**Q28. Your team adopted DORA metrics to measure DevOps performance. You have deployment frequency = 1/week, lead
time = 2 weeks, MTTR = 2 days, change failure rate = 40%. You are classified as low performer. What technical and process
changes move you to Elite (per 2024 DORA report) while maintaining stability?**
A. Deploy less frequently to reduce failures
B. Implement trunk-based development with short-lived branches <1 day, automated testing pyramid with 80% coverage,

C. Increase manual approval gates to 5 managers

**Q29. You run Kubernetes 1.30+ in production. You notice noisy neighbor issues, pods evicted due to memory pressure,
and secrets mounted as env vars leak in logs. What hardening and resource management fixes are required?**
A. Set no resource requests/limits and mount secrets as env vars
B. Set Guaranteed QoS with equal requests/limits for critical pods, use LimitRange and ResourceQuota, enable Kubelet
memory manager and CPU manager policies, use VPA/HPA with KEDA, enforce PodSecurityStandards restricted profile, use
Seccomp/AppArmor, and mount secrets via CSI secret store driver volume mounts not env, plus ephemeral containers for

C. Give every pod privileged access
D. Disable all QoS classes
**Q30. You are designing progressive delivery pipeline for 2026: deploy new service version to 5% of users, auto-promote if
error rate <1% and P95 <250ms, auto-rollback otherwise, with automated canary analysis and mesh traffic splitting. Which
implementation?**
A. Manual kubectl set image and watch logs
B. Use Argo Rollouts or Flagger with Istio/Linkerd / AWS App Mesh / Gateway API for traffic splitting, define
AnalysisTemplate with Prometheus/Datadog queries for success criteria (error rate, latency, custom business metrics),
integrate with progressive delivery and feature flags and use automated promotion/rollback with notifications to Slack
C. Deploy to 100% immediately and hope
D. Use FTP to upload new binary

--- PAGE 166 ---
Advanced Answer Key & Explanations
**Q21 Answer: B - Configure proper lifecycle: distinct readiness probe that fails during shutdown, preStop hook with sleep 15,
terminationGracePeriodSeconds**
Instant SIGTERM kills in-flight requests; readiness removal plus preStop allows LB to deregister and drain connections
gracefully. PDB prevents voluntary disruption evicting all replicas during rollout.
**Q22 Answer: B - Use Trivy/Grype in CI, build with SBOM/provenance, sign with Cosign/Sigstore keyless, enforce with
Kyverno/Gatekeeper**
SLSA Level 3 requires hermetic builds, provenance, and signing; Sigstore keyless OIDC eliminates key management.
Admission controller verifies signature and blocks unsigned or vulnerable images for supply chain security.
**Q23 Answer: B - Use Terraform Cloud remote execution with speculative plans on PR, state locking, Sentinel/OPA
policies**
Remote execution guarantees single apply with locking, speculative plans catch issues early. Policy as code prevents
misconfigurations like public S3, and stack splitting reduces blast radius and plan time at 1000+ resources.
**Q24 Answer: B - Implement multi-window multi-burn-rate alerts and error budget policy**
Multi-window prevents alert flapping by comparing short and long windows for realistic burn detection. Tying error budget
consumption to deployment freeze enforces reliability over velocity per SRE principles.
**Q25 Answer: B - Adopt artifact management with OCI Registry as universal store, immutable tags, SBOM + SLSA Level 3,
Backstage IDP**
OCI registry unification enables tracking via digest and provenance chain from commit SHA to deployed image. SBOM and
SLSA provide auditability and reproducibility, while GC controls cost and sprawl.
**Q26 Answer: C - Use cloud-managed multi-region DB or Patroni with synchronous replication + GitOps ApplicationSets**
Stateful workloads cannot rely on simple pod replication; managed global databases provide synchronous cross-region
guarantees. Separating data and stateless deploys via ApplicationSets enables orchestrated progressive multi-cluster rollout.
**Q27 Answer: B - Move to pull-based config management with AWX/AAP scheduled jobs + Packer immutable AMIs + SSM
State Manager**
Push-only Ansible suffers from drift when manual changes occur. Immutable AMIs prevent drift entirely by rebuilding, while
scheduled pull and compliance tools continuously enforce and report desired state.
**Q28 Answer: B - Implement trunk-based development with short-lived branches, automated testing, feature flags,
ephemeral envs, platform golden paths**
DORA Elite traits correlate with trunk-based, <1 day branch lifetime, and extensive automation. Platform engineering golden
paths reduce toil and enable self-service, improving all four DORA metrics simultaneously.
**Q29 Answer: B - Set Guaranteed QoS, use LimitRange/Quota, memory/cpu manager policies, VPA/HPA KEDA, PSS
restricted, CSI secret store**
Guaranteed QoS ensures scheduling predictability and reduces eviction. Secret-as-volume prevents env leaks in
logs/process listings, and restricted PSS plus Seccomp hardened pod security posture.
**Q30 Answer: B - Use Argo Rollouts or Flagger with Istio/Linkerd / Gateway API for traffic splitting with AnalysisTemplate**
Declarative progressive delivery operators automate canary analysis using metrics queries, enabling automatic
promotion/rollback without manual intervention. Mesh traffic splitting provides fine-grained weight-based routing down to 1%
granularity.
